is bounded, one sees that the martingale part converges to zero in
L^{2}
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{3}}{\mathbb{E}}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{0}b[s,X^{N,i}_{s-},\mu^{N}_{s-}]{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{N,i}_{s}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{0}{\mathbb{E}}|b[s,X^{N,i}_{s},\mu^{N}_{s}]|^{2}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\leqslant\frac{\|b\|^{2}_{\infty}t}{N}.

Therefore, it is not expected that
Z^{N}_{t}
converges to some non-degenerate Gaussian distribution. Moreover, let

a^{N}_{t}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{0}\Big(b[s,X^{N,i}_{s},\mu^{N}_{s}]-b[s,\bar{X}^{i}_{s},\mu_{\bar{X}^{i}_{s}}]\Big){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s.

By (
2.25
), (
2.28
) and Theorem
2.18
, it is easy to see that for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|a^{N}_{t}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant T\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int^{T}_{0}{\mathbb{E}}\Big|b[s,X^{N,i}_{s},\mu^{N}_{s}]-b[s,\bar{X}^{i}_{s},\mu_{\bar{X}^{i}_{s}}]\Big|^{2}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\leqslant C_{T},

where
C_{T}
does not depend on
N
.
We aim to show the following result about the fluctuation.

Theorem 2.19
.

Suppose that (
2.25
) and (
2.26
) hold. Then as
N\to\infty
,

\sqrt{N}(Z^{N}_{t}-a^{N}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}\Rightarrow(Y_{t})_{t\geqslant 0},

where
Y_{t}=\int^{t}_{0}b{\big(}s,X_{s},\mu_{X_{s}}{\big)}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}W_{s}
is a Gaussian martingale, and

X_{t}
solves the following DDODE:

\displaystyle X_{t}=X_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}b(s,X_{s},\mu_{X_{s}}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,

(2.30)

and
X_{0}\sim\nu
, is the common distribution of
X^{N,i}_{0}
, and
W
is a one dimensional standard Brownian motion.

Proof.

By definition it is easy to see that

\displaystyle Y^{N}_{t}:=\sqrt{N}(Z^{N}_{t}-a^{N}_{t})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(X^{N,i}_{t}-\int^{t}_{0}b[s,X^{N,i}_{s},\mu^{N}_{s}]{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{0}{\mathcal{A}}^{N,i}_{s}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{N,i}_{s},

(2.31)

where

{\mathcal{A}}^{N,i}_{s}:=b[s,X^{N,i}_{s-},\mu^{N}_{s-}]/N.

For any stopping time
\tau
and
\delta>0
, by Doob’s maximal inequality we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}|Y^{N}_{\tau+t}-Y^{N}_{\tau}|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant 4N\sum_{i=1}^{N}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\tau+\delta}_{\tau}|{\mathcal{A}}^{N,i}_{s}|^{2}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)\leqslant 4\|b\|_{\infty}^{2}\delta.

To prove the result, we consider an auxiliary process
\widetilde{X}_{t}
, which satisfies (
2.30
) with starting point
\widetilde{X}_{0}
independent of
X^{i}_{0}
. Clearly, we also have

\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}|\widetilde{X}_{\tau+t}-\widetilde{X}_{\tau}|\leqslant\|b\|_{\infty}\delta.

Thus by Aldous’ criterion (see
[
23
, p356, Theorem 4.5]
), the law
{\mathbb{P}}_{N}
of
(\widetilde{X}_{\cdot},Y^{N}_{\cdot})_{N\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
{\mathbb{D}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
is tight.
Without loss of generality, we assume that
{\mathbb{P}}_{N}
weakly converges to
{\mathbb{P}}_{\infty}
.
We show that
{\mathbb{P}}_{\infty}
is a martingale solution of the following second order operator starting from
\nu\otimes\delta_{0}
at time
0

{\mathscr{L}}_{s}f(x,y)=b[s,x,\mu_{s}]\cdot\nabla_{x}f(x,y)+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}{\big(}(b\otimes b)[s,x,\mu_{s}]\cdot\nabla^{2}_{y}f(x,y){\big)}.

For
f\in C^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
, we need to show that for
w_{t}=(x_{t},y_{t})\in{\mathbb{D}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
,

f(w_{t})-f(w_{0})-\int^{t}_{0}{\mathscr{L}}_{s}f(w_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,

is a
{\mathbb{P}}_{\infty}
-martingale. On one hand, let

{\mathscr{E}}:=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}h_{i}(x)g_{i}(y),\ h_{i},g_{i}\in C^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),a_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}},m\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}.

Since
{\mathscr{E}}
is dense in
C^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
,
it suffices to consider
f(x,y)=h(x)g(y)
, where
h,g\in C^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.
On the other hand, since
\widetilde{X}
solves ODE (
2.29
), we have

h(\widetilde{X}_{t})=h(\widetilde{X}_{0})+\int^{t}_{0}b[s,\widetilde{X}_{s},\mu_{s}]\cdot\nabla h(\widetilde{X}_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s.

Therefore, we only need to consider
f(x,y)=g(y)
.
By (
2.31
) and Itô’s formula, we have

g(Y^{N}_{t})=g(0)+\int^{t}_{0}{\mathscr{A}}^{N}_{s}g(Y^{N}_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+M^{N}_{t},

where
M^{N}_{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{0}\Big(g(Y^{N}_{s-}+{\mathcal{A}}^{N,i}_{s})-g(Y^{N}_{s-})\Big){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{N,i}_{s}
is a martingale, and

{\mathscr{A}}^{N}_{s}g(y):=N\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Big(g(y+\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{N,i}_{s})-g(y)-\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{N,i}_{s}\cdot\nabla g(y)\Big),

and

\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{N,i}_{s}:=b[s,X^{N,i}_{s},\mu^{N}_{s}]/N.

Below, for simplicity of notations, we write

B_{s}(x,y):=(b\otimes b)(s,x,y),\ \ {\mathscr{A}}_{s}g(y):=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}{\big(}B_{s}[\widetilde{X}_{s},\mu_{s}]\cdot\nabla^{2}g(y){\big)}.

By Theorem
6.4
, it suffices to show

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\int^{T}_{0}\Big|{\mathbb{E}}{\mathscr{A}}^{N}_{s}g(Y^{N}_{s})-{\mathbb{E}}{\mathscr{A}}_{s}g(Y^{N}_{s})\Big|{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s=0.

Observe that by Taylor’s expansion,

{\mathscr{A}}^{N}_{s}g(y)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathrm{tr}\left(B_{s}[X^{N,i}_{s},\mu^{N}_{s}]\cdot\int^{1}_{0}\theta\int^{1}_{0}\nabla^{2}_{y}g(y+\theta\theta^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{N,i}_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta^{\prime}\right).

Let

\bar{\mathscr{A}}^{N}_{s}g(y):=\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathrm{tr}\big(B_{s}[\bar{X}^{i}_{s},\mu_{s}]\cdot\nabla^{2}g(y)\big)=\mathrm{tr}{\big(}B_{s}[\mu_{\bar{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s}},\mu_{s}]\cdot\nabla^{2}g(y){\big)}.

Then

{\mathscr{A}}^{N}_{s}g(y)-{\mathscr{A}}_{s}g(y)={\mathscr{A}}^{N}_{s}g(y)-\bar{\mathscr{A}}^{N}_{s}g(y)+\bar{\mathscr{A}}^{N}_{s}g(y)-{\mathscr{A}}_{s}g(y)

By Theorem
2.18
, it is easy to see that

\sup_{s\in[0,T]}{\mathbb{E}}\|{\mathscr{A}}^{N}_{s}g-\bar{\mathscr{A}}^{N}_{s}g\|^{2}_{\infty}\leqslant C/N.

Moreover, since
\mu_{\bar{X}^{i}_{s}}=\mu_{s}
, by (
2.28
), we also have

\displaystyle\left|{\mathbb{E}}\int^{t}_{0}\Big(\bar{\mathscr{A}}^{N}_{s}g{\big(}Y^{N}_{s}{\big)}-{\mathscr{A}}_{s}g{\big(}Y^{N}_{s}{\big)}\Big){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right|^{2}\leqslant t\int^{t}_{0}\left|{\mathbb{E}}\bar{\mathscr{A}}^{N}_{s}g{\big(}Y^{N}_{s}{\big)}-{\mathbb{E}}{\mathscr{A}}_{s}g{\big(}Y^{N}_{s}{\big)}\right|^{2}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{t}{2}\int^{t}_{0}\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(\mathrm{tr}\big((B_{s}[\mu_{\bar{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s}},\mu_{s}]-{\mathbb{E}}B_{s}[\widetilde{X}_{s},\mu_{s}])\cdot\nabla^{2}g(Y^{N}_{s}){\big)}\right)\right|^{2}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

\displaystyle\qquad\leqslant\frac{t}{2}\|\nabla^{2}g\|_{\infty}\int^{t}_{0}{\mathbb{E}}\left|B_{s}[\mu_{\bar{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s}},\mu_{s}]-{\mathbb{E}}B_{s}[\widetilde{X}_{s},\mu_{s}]\right|^{2}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\leqslant\frac{C}{N}.

Hence,

{\mathbb{E}}\left|\int^{t}_{0}({\mathscr{L}}^{N}_{s}f-{\mathscr{L}}_{s}f)(\widetilde{X}_{s},Y^{N}_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right|^{2}\leqslant\frac{C}{N}.

Thus, by Theorem
6.4
in appendix, we get
{\mathbb{P}}_{\infty}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\nu\otimes\delta_{0}}_{0}({\mathscr{L}})
and conclude the proof.
∎

Remark 2.20
.

By the above theorem, one sees that
(\sqrt{N}(Z^{N}_{t}-a^{N}_{t}))_{t\in[0,T]}
weakly converges to a Gaussian martingale
with covariance matrix
\int^{t}_{0}(b\otimes b)(s,X_{s},\mu_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s
.

3.
Compound Poisson approximation for SDEs

The main objective of this section is to introduce a unified compound Poisson approximation for SDEs driven by either Brownian motions or
\alpha
-stable processes.
This is accomplished by selecting different scaling parameters. We establish the convergence of the approximation SDEs under relatively mild assumptions, as demonstrated in Theorem
3.16
. Furthermore, under more restrictive assumptions, we derive the convergence rate in Theorem
3.19
. Additionally, we obtain the convergence of the invariant measures under dissipativity assumptions, as presented in Theorem
3.17
. The convergence of the generators plays a pivotal role in our proofs.
In essence, our results can be interpreted as a form of nonlinear central limit theorem. In the subsequent section, we will apply this framework to address nonlinear partial differential equations (PDEs),
with a specific focus on the 2D-Navier-Stokes equations on the torus.

Let
(\xi_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a sequence of i.i.d.
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
-valued symmetric random variables with common distribution
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.
Let
\xi_{0}=0
. For
\varepsilon>0
, we define a compound Poisson process
H^{\varepsilon}
by

\displaystyle H^{\varepsilon}_{t}:=\sum_{n\leqslant{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}\xi_{n},\ \ t\geqslant 0,

(3.1)

where
{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}
is the Poisson process with intensity
1/\varepsilon
(see (
2.1
)). Let
{\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}
be the associated Poisson random measure, i.e.,
for
t>0
and
E\in{\mathscr{B}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}([0,t],E):=\sum_{s\leqslant t}{\mathbbm{1}}_{E}(\Delta H^{\varepsilon}_{s})=\sum_{n\leqslant{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{E}(\xi_{n}),

(3.2)

where
\Delta H^{\varepsilon}_{s}:=H^{\varepsilon}_{s}-H^{\varepsilon}_{s-}
. More precisely, for a function
f(s,z):{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,

\displaystyle\int^{t}_{0}f(s,z){\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z):=\sum_{s\leqslant t}f(s,\Delta H^{\varepsilon}_{s}){\mathbbm{1}}_{\{\Delta{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{s}=1\}}=\sum_{n\leqslant{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}f(S^{\varepsilon}_{n},\xi_{n}),

(3.3)

where
S^{\varepsilon}_{n}=\varepsilon S_{n}
is the
n
-th jump time of
{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}
.
Note that the compensated measure of
{\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}
is given by
{\mathord{{\rm d}}}t\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)/\varepsilon
. We also write

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}([0,t],E):={\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}([0,t],E)-t\nu(E)/\varepsilon,

which is called the compensated Poisson random measure of
{\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}
.

Fix
\alpha>0
. We make the following assumptions for the probability measure
\nu
above:

(
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
)

\nu
is symmetric, i.e.,
\nu(-{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)
.
If
\alpha\geqslant 2
, we suppose that

\nu(|z|^{\alpha}):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|z|^{\alpha}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)<\infty.

If
\alpha\in(0,2)
, we suppose that

\displaystyle\sup_{\lambda\geqslant 1}\left[\lambda^{\alpha-2}\int_{|z|\leqslant\lambda}|z|^{2}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)+\lambda^{\alpha}\int_{|z|>\lambda}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right]<\infty,

(3.4)

and there is a Lévy measure
\nu_{0}
and constants
\beta_{0}\in[0,1]
,
\beta_{1},c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that for any measurable function
G:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying

\displaystyle|G(z)|\leqslant c_{1}(|z|^{2}\wedge 1),\ \ |G(z)-G(z^{\prime})|\leqslant c_{1}(|z-z^{\prime}|\wedge 1)^{\beta_{0}},

(3.5)

it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}G(z)\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}G(z)\nu_{0}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right|\leqslant c_{1}c_{2}\varepsilon^{\beta_{1}},\ \forall\varepsilon\in(0,1),

(3.6)

where

\displaystyle\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z):=\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z/\varepsilon^{1/\alpha})/\varepsilon.

(3.7)

Remark 3.1
.

If
\beta_{0}=0
in (
3.5
), then (
3.6
) means that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}(|z|^{2}\wedge 1)|\nu_{\varepsilon}-\nu_{0}|({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\leqslant c_{1}c_{2}\varepsilon^{\beta_{1}},

where
|\nu_{\varepsilon}-\nu_{0}|({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)
stands for the total variation measure. Examples 1 and 2 below correspond to
\beta_{0}=0
and
\beta_{1}=\frac{2}{\alpha}-1
.
For
\beta_{0}>0
, condition (
3.6
) is used in Example 3 below.

In the following we provide several examples for
\alpha\in(0,2)
to illustrate the above assumptions.

Example 1.

Let
\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=c_{0}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{C}}\cap B^{c}_{1}}(z)|z|^{-d-\alpha}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z
with
\alpha\in(0,2)
, where
{\mathcal{C}}
is a cone with vertex
0
and
c_{0}

is a normalized constant so that
\nu({\mathbb{R}}^{d})=1
. It is easy to see that (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
) holds with

\nu_{0}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=c_{0}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}(z)|z|^{-d-\alpha}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z
and
\beta_{0}=0
,
\beta_{1}=\frac{2}{\alpha}-1
. In this case

(\nu_{\varepsilon}-\nu_{0})({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=c_{0}{\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{C}}\cap B_{\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}}(z)}|z|^{-d-\alpha}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z
. In particular, if
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, then
up to a constant,
\nu_{0}
is just the Lévy measure of a
rotationally invariant and symmetric
\alpha
-stable process.

Example 2.

Let
\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=c_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{d}{\mathbbm{1}}_{|z_{i}|>1}|z_{i}|^{-1-\alpha}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z_{i}\delta_{\{0\}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z^{*}_{i})
with
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
where
c_{0}
is a constant so that
\nu({\mathbb{R}}^{d})=1
and
z^{*}_{i}
denotes the remaining variables except
z_{i}
.
It is easy to see that (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
) holds with

\nu_{0}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=c_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{d}|z_{i}|^{-1-\alpha}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z_{i}\delta_{\{0\}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z^{*}_{i})
and
\beta_{0}=0
,
\beta_{1}=\frac{2}{\alpha}-1
.
In this case,
\nu_{0}
is a cylindrical Lévy measure.

Example 3.
Let
\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=c_{0}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}}|k|^{-1-\alpha}\delta_{k}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)
with
\alpha\in(0,2)
, where
c_{0}
is a constant so that
\nu({\mathbb{R}})=1
. First of all it is easy to see that (
3.4
) holds. We now
verify that (
3.6
) holds for
\nu_{0}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=c_{0}|z|^{-1-\alpha}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z
and
\beta_{0}\in(0,1]
and
\beta_{1}<(1-\frac{\alpha}{2})\beta_{0}
. Note that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}G(z)\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=c_{0}\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{G(k\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{\alpha}})}{\varepsilon|k|^{1+\alpha}}=c_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{G(z_{\varepsilon})}{|z_{\varepsilon}|^{1+\alpha}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z,

where
z_{\varepsilon}=\mbox{\rm sgn}(z)[|z|\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}]\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}
, and
[a]
denotes the integer part of a real number
a>0
.
Here we have used the convention
\frac{0}{0}=0
. Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c_{0}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}G(z)\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}G(z)\nu_{0}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{|z|<2\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\left|\frac{G(z_{\varepsilon})}{|z_{\varepsilon}|^{1+\alpha}}-\frac{G(z)}{|z|^{1+\alpha}}\right|{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z

\displaystyle+\int_{|z|\geqslant 2\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}|G(z_{\varepsilon})|\left|\frac{1}{|z_{\varepsilon}|^{1+\alpha}}-\frac{1}{|z|^{1+\alpha}}\right|{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z

\displaystyle+\int_{|z|\geqslant 2\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\frac{|G(z_{\varepsilon})-G(z)|}{|z|^{1+\alpha}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z

\displaystyle=:I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}.

For
I_{1}
, by (
3.5
) we clearly have

I_{1}\leqslant c_{1}\int_{|z|<2\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\Big(|z_{\varepsilon}|^{1-\alpha}+\frac{|z|^{2}}{|z|^{1+\alpha}}\Big){\mathord{{\rm d}}}z\lesssim c_{1}\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{\alpha}-1}.

Since
|z_{\varepsilon}-z|\leqslant\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}
, we have for
|z|\geqslant 2\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}
,

|z|/2\leqslant|z_{\varepsilon}|\leqslant 2|z|,

and

\left|\frac{1}{|z_{\varepsilon}|^{1+\alpha}}-\frac{1}{|z|^{1+\alpha}}\right|\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}{|z|^{2+\alpha}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim c_{1}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\int_{|z|\geqslant 2\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\frac{|z|^{2}\wedge 1}{|z|^{2+\alpha}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z\lesssim\left\{\begin{aligned} &c_{1}\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{\alpha}-1},&\alpha\in(1,2),\\
&c_{1}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}|\log\varepsilon|,&\alpha=1,\\
&c_{1}\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{\alpha}},&\alpha\in(0,1).\end{aligned}\right.

For
I_{3}
, noting that by (
3.5
),

|G(z_{\varepsilon})-G(z)|\leqslant c_{1}(|z|^{\alpha}\wedge 1)\varepsilon^{(1-\frac{\alpha}{2})\beta_{0}},

we have

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim c_{1}\varepsilon^{(1-\frac{\alpha}{2})\beta_{0}}\int_{|z|\geqslant 2\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\frac{|z|^{\alpha}\wedge 1}{|z|^{1+\alpha}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z\lesssim c_{1}\varepsilon^{(1-\frac{\alpha}{2})\beta_{0}}|\log\varepsilon|.

Combining the above calculations, we obtain (
3.6
) for any
\beta_{0}\in(0,1]
and
\beta_{1}<(1-\frac{\alpha}{2})\beta_{0}
.

Remark 3.2
.

For the above examples, one sees that for
\alpha\in(0,2)
,

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|z|^{\alpha}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=\infty,\ \ \int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|z|^{\beta}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)<\infty,\ \ \beta\in[0,\alpha).

The following lemma is useful.

Lemma 3.3
.

Under (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
), for
\alpha\in(0,2)
and
\beta\in[0,\alpha)
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\lambda\geqslant 1,\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\left[\lambda^{\alpha-2}\int_{|z|\leqslant\lambda}|z|^{2}\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)+\lambda^{\alpha-\beta}\int_{|z|>\lambda}|z|^{\beta}\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right]<\infty,

(3.8)

where
\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z):=\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z/\varepsilon^{1/\alpha})/\varepsilon.

Proof.

First of all, by (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{|z|>\lambda}|z|^{\beta}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{2^{k}\lambda\leqslant|z|<2^{k+1}\lambda}|z|^{\beta}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{(k+1)\beta}\lambda^{\beta}\int_{2^{k}\lambda\leqslant|z|<2^{k+1}\lambda}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{(k+1)\beta}\lambda^{\beta}2^{-k\alpha}\lambda^{-\alpha}\leqslant C\lambda^{\beta-\alpha}.

The desired estimate follows by the change of variables.
∎

Now, we introduce a general approximating scheme for SDEs driven by either Brownian motions or
\alpha
-stable processes.
Let
\sigma_{\varepsilon}(t,x,z):{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
b_{\varepsilon}(t,x):{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, where

\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, be two families of Borel measurable functions. Suppose that

\sigma_{\varepsilon}(t,x,-z)=-\sigma_{\varepsilon}(t,x,z).

Note that the above assumption implies that

\sigma_{\varepsilon}(t,x,0)\equiv 0.

Consider the following SDE driven by compound Poisson process
{\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}X^{\varepsilon}_{t}&=X^{\varepsilon}_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\Big(\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-},z)+b_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-})\Big){\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\\
&=X^{\varepsilon}_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-},z){\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)+\int^{t}_{0}b_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{s}\\
&=X^{\varepsilon}_{0}+\sum_{s\leqslant t}\Big(\sigma_{\varepsilon}{\big(}s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-},\Delta H^{\varepsilon}_{s}{\big)}+b_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-})\Delta{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{s}\Big).\end{split}

(3.9)

Note that
H^{\varepsilon}_{s}
and
{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{s}
jump simultaneously, that is,
\Delta H^{\varepsilon}_{s}\not=0
if and only if

\Delta{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{s}=1
.
In particular,

X^{\varepsilon}_{t}-X^{\varepsilon}_{t-}=\sigma_{\varepsilon}{\big(}t,X^{\varepsilon}_{t-},\Delta H^{\varepsilon}_{t}{\big)}+b_{\varepsilon}(t,X^{\varepsilon}_{t-})\Delta{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}.

Moreover, by the symmetry of
\nu
and
\sigma_{\varepsilon}(t,x,-z)=-\sigma_{\varepsilon}(t,x,z)
,

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-},z)\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=0,

(3.10)

we thus can write SDE (
3.9
) as the following form:

\displaystyle X^{\varepsilon}_{t}=X^{\varepsilon}_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}b_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\big(}\tfrac{s}{\varepsilon}{\big)}+\int^{t}_{0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\Big(\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-},z)+b_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-})\Big)\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z),

(3.11)

where the last term is the stochastic integral with respect to the compensated Poisson random measure
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}
, which is a local càdlàg martingale.

Without any conditions on
\sigma
and
b
, SDE (
3.9
) is always solvable since there are only finite terms in the summation of (
3.9
) and it can be solved recursively.
In fact, we have the following explicit construction for the solution of SDE (
3.9
).

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{0}\equiv X^{\varepsilon}_{0}
. For
n=0,1,2,\cdots
, we define
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{n}
recursively by

\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{n+1}:=\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{n}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}{\big(}S^{\varepsilon}_{n+1},\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{n},\xi_{n+1}{\big)}+b_{\varepsilon}{\big(}S^{\varepsilon}_{n+1},\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{n}{\big)},

where
S^{\varepsilon}_{n}=\varepsilon S_{n}
.
Then
(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is a Markov chain, and for any
t\geqslant 0
,

X^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}.

Proof.

It is direct by definitions (
3.9
) and (
3.3
).
∎

Based on the above lemma, we have the following algorithm.

(1)

Fix a step
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and iteration number
N
.

(2)

Initialize
S^{\varepsilon}_{0}=0
and
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{0}=X^{\varepsilon}_{0}
. Let
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
satisfy (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
).

(3)

Generate
N
-i.i.d. random variables
(T_{n})\sim{\rm Exp}(1)
and
(\xi_{n})\sim\nu
.

(4)

For
n=0
to
N-1

S^{\varepsilon}_{n+1}=S^{\varepsilon}_{n}+\varepsilon*T_{n+1}
;
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{n+1}=\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{n}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}(S^{\varepsilon}_{n+1},\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{n},\xi_{n+1})+b_{\varepsilon}(S^{\varepsilon}_{n+1},\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{n})
.

(5)

For given
t>0
, let
{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}:=\max\{n:S^{\varepsilon}_{n}\leqslant t\}
and output
X^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}\wedge N}
.

The following simple lemma provides a tail probability estimate for
{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}
, which informs us on how to choose the value of
N
in practice.

Lemma 3.5
.

For any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have

{\mathbb{P}}{\big(}{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}\geqslant\tfrac{(\mathrm{e}-1)t}{\varepsilon}+n{\big)}\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-n}.

Proof.

By Chebyschev’s inequality we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}{\big(}{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}\geqslant\tfrac{(\mathrm{e}-1)t}{\varepsilon}+n{\big)}\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(\mathrm{e}-1)t}{\varepsilon}-n}{\mathbb{E}}\mathrm{e}^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}}=\mathrm{e}^{-n}.

∎

Remark 3.6
.

The sequence
(\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{n})_{n\geqslant 0}
forms a Markov chain with a state space of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. These lemmas provide us with a practical method for simulating
X^{\varepsilon}_{t}
using a computer. It is important to note that approximating a diffusion process with a Markov chain is a well-established topic, as discussed in
[
38
, Chapter 11.2]
. Therein, the focus is on the time-homogeneous case, and piecewise linear interpolation is used for approximation. In our approach, we embed the Markov chain into a continuous process using a Poisson process. It is crucial to highlight that
\Gamma^{\varepsilon}_{n}
is not independent of
\mathcal{N}^{\varepsilon}_{t}
due to the time-inhomogeneous nature of
\sigma
and
b
. Our computations heavily rely on the calculus of stochastic integrals with jumps.

Note that for a bounded measurable function
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,

\displaystyle f(X^{\varepsilon}_{t})-f(X_{0})=\sum_{s\leqslant t}f(X^{\varepsilon}_{s})-f(X^{\varepsilon}_{s-})

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{s\leqslant t}\left(f{\big(}X^{\varepsilon}_{s-}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}{\big(}s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-},\Delta H^{\varepsilon}_{s}{\big)}+b_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-})\Delta{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{s}{\big)}-f(X^{\varepsilon}_{s-})\right)

\displaystyle\quad\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{DSQ1}}}{{=}}\int^{t}_{0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left(f(X^{\varepsilon}_{s-}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-},z)+b_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-}))-f(X^{\varepsilon}_{s-})\right){\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle\quad=\int^{t}_{0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{f(X^{\varepsilon}_{s}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s},z)+b_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s}))-f(X^{\varepsilon}_{s})}{\varepsilon}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+M^{\varepsilon}_{t},

(3.12)

where
M^{\varepsilon}_{t}
is a martingale defined by

M^{\varepsilon}_{t}:=\int^{t}_{0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left(f{\big(}X^{\varepsilon}_{s-}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-},z)+b_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-}){\big)}-f(X^{\varepsilon}_{s-})\right)\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z).

This is just the Itô formula of jump processes. In particular,

{\mathbb{E}}f(X^{\varepsilon}_{t})-{\mathbb{E}}f(X_{0})={\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{t}_{0}{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(X^{\varepsilon}_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right),

where the infinitesimal generator
{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}
of Markov process
(X^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
is given by

{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{f(x+\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,x,z)+b_{\varepsilon}(s,x))-f(x)}{\varepsilon}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=:{\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x)+{\mathcal{B}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x)

with

{\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x+\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,x,z))-{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x)}{\varepsilon}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x):=\frac{{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x)-f(x)}{\varepsilon},\ \ {\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x):=f(x+b_{\varepsilon}(s,x)).

(3.13)

By convention we have used that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x+y)=f(x+y+b_{\varepsilon}(s,x)).

(3.14)

Note that by the symmetry of
\nu
and
\sigma(t,x,-z)=-\sigma(t,x,z)
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x+\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,x,z))+{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x-\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,x,z))-{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x)}{2\varepsilon}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z).

(3.15)

The concrete choices of
\sigma_{\varepsilon}
(depending on
\alpha
) and
b_{\varepsilon}
will be given in the following subsection.

3.1.
Weak convergence of approximating SDEs

In this section, our aim is to construct appropriate functions
\sigma_{\varepsilon}
and
b_{\varepsilon}
such that the law of the approximating SDE converges to the law of the classical SDE driven by
\alpha
-stable processes or Brownian motions. The key aspect of our construction lies in demonstrating the convergence of the generators. It is important to note that the drift term is assumed to satisfy dissipativity conditions and can exhibit polynomial growth.

Let

\sigma:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\ b:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

be two Borel measurable functions. We make the following assumptions on
\sigma
and
b
:

(
H
{}^{\sigma}_{b}
)

\sigma(t,x,z)
and
b(t,x)
are locally bounded and continuous in
x
, and for some
\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1}>0
,

\displaystyle\sigma(t,x,-z)=-\sigma(t,x,z),\ \ |\sigma(t,x,z)|\leqslant(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}|x|)|z|,

(3.16)

and for the same
\beta_{0}
as in (
3.5
),

\displaystyle|\sigma(t,x,z)-\sigma(t,x,z^{\prime})|\leqslant(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}|x|)(|z-z^{\prime}|\wedge 1)^{\beta_{0}},

(3.17)

and for some
m\geqslant 1
,
\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4}\geqslant 0
and
\kappa_{5}<0
,

\displaystyle|b(t,x)|\leqslant(\kappa_{2}(1+|x|))^{m},\ \ \langle x,b(t,x)\rangle\leqslant\kappa_{3}+\kappa_{4}|x|^{2}+\kappa_{5}|x|^{m+1}.

(3.18)

We introduce the coefficients of the approximating SDE (
3.9
) by

\displaystyle b_{\varepsilon}(t,x):=\frac{\varepsilon b(t,x)}{1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}|b(t,x)|^{1-\frac{1}{m}}},\ \ \sigma_{\varepsilon}(t,x,z):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma(t,x,z),&\ \alpha\geqslant 2,\\
&\sigma(t,x,\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}z),&\ \alpha\in(0,2).\end{aligned}\right.

(3.19)

Remark 3.7
.

The purpose of introducing the function
b_{\varepsilon}
is to ensure the dissipativity of the approximating SDEs, as demonstrated in Lemma
3.11
below. On the other hand, the introduction of
\sigma_{\varepsilon}
with different scaling parameters for different values of
\alpha
is aimed at ensuring the convergence of the generators, as shown in Lemma
3.9
below. It is worth noting that the drift term
b
can exhibit polynomial growth, and in the case of linear growth (i.e.,
m=1
), one can simply choose
b_{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\varepsilon b(t,x)
. Furthermore, by the definition of
b_{\varepsilon}
, it is evident that

\displaystyle|b_{\varepsilon}(t,x)|\leqslant(\varepsilon|b(t,x)|)\wedge(\sqrt{\varepsilon}|b(t,x)|^{\frac{1}{m}}).

(3.20)

In the next lemma we shall show that as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x)
converges to
{\mathscr{L}}^{(0)}_{s}f(x)
with

\displaystyle{\mathscr{L}}^{(0)}_{s}f(x)={\mathcal{A}}^{(0)}_{s}f(x)+b(s,x)\cdot\nabla f(x),

(3.21)

where

{\mathcal{A}}^{(0)}_{s}f(x):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sigma(s,x,z)\otimes\sigma(s,x,z)\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\cdot\nabla^{2}f(x)\right),&\alpha\geqslant 2,\\
&\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{f(x+\sigma(s,x,z))+f(x-\sigma(s,x,z))-2f(x)}{2}\nu_{0}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z),&\alpha\in(0,2).\end{aligned}\right.

This observation suggests that
X^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot}
is expected to weakly converge to a solution of the following SDE:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &{\mathord{{\rm d}}}X_{t}=\sigma_{\nu}^{(2)}{\big(}t,X_{t}{\big)}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}W_{t}+b(t,X_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t,&\alpha\geqslant 2,\\
&{\mathord{{\rm d}}}X_{t}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sigma{\big(}t,X_{t-},z{\big)}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}t,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)+b(t,X_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t,&\alpha\in(0,2),\end{aligned}\right.

(3.22)

where
W_{t}
is a
d
-dimensional standard Brownian motion, and

\sigma_{\nu}^{(2)}(t,x):=\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sigma(t,x,z)\otimes\sigma(t,x,z)\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

and when
\alpha\in(0,2)
, for a
d
-dimensional symmetric Lévy process
L^{(\alpha)}_{t}
with Lévy measure
\nu_{0}
,

{\mathcal{H}}([0,t]\times E):=\sum_{s\leqslant t}{\mathbbm{1}}_{E}(\Delta L^{(\alpha)}_{s}),\ t\geqslant 0,E\in{\mathscr{B}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),

and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}([0,t]\times E):={\mathcal{H}}([0,t]\times E)-t\nu_{0}(E),\ t\geqslant 0,E\in{\mathscr{B}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}).

(3.23)

Remark 3.8
.

Let
\alpha\geqslant 2
and
\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=d^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\bar{\nu}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z_{i})\delta_{\{0\}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z^{*}_{i})
,
where
\bar{\nu}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}})
satisfies
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|z|^{\alpha}\bar{\nu}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)<\infty
, and
z^{*}_{i}
represents the remaining variables except for
z_{i}
.
Let

\sigma(t,x,z)=\sigma_{0}(t,x)z
, where
\sigma_{0}(t,x):{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\otimes{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is Borel measurable.
In this case, we can take

\sigma_{\nu}^{(2)}(t,x)=\sigma_{0}(t,x)\sqrt{\bar{\nu}(|z|^{2})/d}.

Let
\{e_{i},i=1,\cdots,d\}
be the canonical basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Suppose that
\sigma(t,x,z)=\sqrt{2d}\cdot z
,
b=0
and

\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=\frac{1}{2d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Big(\delta_{e_{i}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)+\delta_{-e_{i}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\Big).

Then
{\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x)=\Delta_{\varepsilon}f(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{f(x+\sqrt{2d\varepsilon}e_{i})+f(x-\sqrt{2d\varepsilon}e_{i})-2f(x)}{2d\varepsilon}
is the standard discrete Laplacian.

The following lemma is crucial for taking limits.

Lemma 3.9
.

Under (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
) and (
H
{}^{\sigma}_{b}
), for any
R>0
, there is a constant
C_{R}>0
such that
for any
f\in C^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, and for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
s\geqslant 0
and
|x|\leqslant R
,

\big|{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x)-{\mathscr{L}}^{(0)}_{s}f(x)\big|\leqslant C_{R}\Big(o(\varepsilon){\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha=2}+\varepsilon^{\frac{(\alpha-2)\wedge 1}{2}}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}_{b}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha>2}+\varepsilon^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}\wedge\beta_{1}}\|f\|_{C^{2}_{b}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha<2}\Big),

where
\beta_{1}
is from (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
).
Moreover, if
b
is bounded measurable and
\kappa_{1}=0
in (
H
{}^{\sigma}_{b}
), then the constant
C_{R}
can be independent of
R>0
.

Proof.

Below we drop the time variable for simplicity.
Recalling
{\mathcal{B}}^{(\varepsilon)}f(x)=\frac{f(x+b_{\varepsilon}(x))-f(x)}{\varepsilon}
, by Taylor’s expansion and the definition (
3.19
), we have

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{B}}^{(\varepsilon)}f(x)-b(x)\cdot\nabla f(x)|

\displaystyle\leqslant|{\mathcal{B}}^{(\varepsilon)}f(x)-\varepsilon^{-1}b_{\varepsilon}(x)\cdot\nabla f(x)|+|(\varepsilon^{-1}b_{\varepsilon}(x)-b(x))\cdot\nabla f(x)|

\displaystyle\leqslant|b_{\varepsilon}(x)|\int^{1}_{0}\frac{|\nabla f(x+\theta b_{\varepsilon}(x))-\nabla f(x)|}{\varepsilon}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta+|\varepsilon^{-1}b_{\varepsilon}(x)-b(x)|\|\nabla f\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon^{-1}|b_{\varepsilon}(x)|^{2}\|\nabla^{2}f\|_{\infty}+\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}|b(x)|^{2-\frac{1}{m}}}{1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}|b(x)|^{1-\frac{1}{m}}}\|\nabla f\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon|b(x)|^{2}\|\nabla^{2}f\|_{\infty}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}|b(x)|^{2-\frac{1}{m}}\|\nabla f\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\sqrt{\varepsilon}\big(1+|b(x)|^{2}\big)\|\nabla f\|_{C^{1}_{b}}.

(3.24)

Next, by (
3.14
) and Taylor’s expansion again, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}f(x+\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z))+{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}f(x-\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z))-2{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}f(x)

\displaystyle\quad=\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z)\cdot\int^{1}_{0}\Big[{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}\nabla f(x+\theta\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z))-{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}\nabla f(x-\theta\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z))\Big]{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta

\displaystyle\quad=\int^{1}_{0}\theta\int^{1}_{-1}\Big[\mathrm{tr}{\big(}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}\otimes\sigma_{\varepsilon})(x,z)\cdot{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}\nabla^{2}f(x+\theta^{\prime}\theta\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z)){\big)}\Big]{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta^{\prime}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta.

(3.25)

When
\alpha\geqslant 2
, recalling
\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z)=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma(x,z)
, by (
3.15
) and (
3.25
) we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}f(x)-{\mathcal{A}}^{(0)}f(x)={\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}f(x)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\mathrm{tr}((\sigma\otimes\sigma)(x,z)\cdot\nabla^{2}f(x))\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int^{1}_{0}\frac{\theta}{2}\int^{1}_{-1}\Big[\mathrm{tr}{\big(}(\sigma\otimes\sigma)(x,z)\cdot{\big(}{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}\nabla^{2}f(x+\theta^{\prime}\theta\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma(x,z))-{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}\nabla^{2}f(x){\big)}{\big)}\Big]{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta^{\prime}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\mathrm{tr}{\big(}(\sigma\otimes\sigma)(x,z)\cdot({\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}\nabla^{2}f(x)-\nabla^{2}f(x)){\big)}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z).

Hence, recalling
{\mathcal{D}}^{\varepsilon}f(x)=f(x+b_{\varepsilon}(x))
, by (
3.16
), we have for
\alpha=2
,

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leqslant R}\big|{\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}f(x)-{\mathcal{A}}^{(0)}f(x)\big|\leqslant C_{R}\,o(\varepsilon),

(3.26)

and for
\alpha>2
,

\displaystyle\big|{\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}f(x)-{\mathcal{A}}^{(0)}f(x)\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{R}\Big(\varepsilon^{\frac{(\alpha-2)\wedge 1}{2}}\nu(|z|^{\alpha})+\varepsilon^{(\alpha-2)\wedge 1}\nu(|z|^{2})\Big)\|\nabla^{2}f\|_{C^{(\alpha-2)\wedge 1}_{b}}.

(3.27)

When
\alpha\in(0,2)
, recalling
\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z)=\sigma(x,\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}z)
and
by (
3.15
) and the change of variables, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}f(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}f(x+\sigma(x,z))+{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}f(x-\sigma(x,z))-2{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}f(x)}{2}\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z),

(3.28)

where

\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z/\varepsilon^{1/\alpha})/\varepsilon.

Hence, for
f_{\varepsilon}:={\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}f-f
, we have

\displaystyle\left|{\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x)-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{f(x+\sigma(x,z))+f(x-\sigma(x,z))-2f(x)}{2}\nu_{0}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{f(x+\sigma(x,z))+f(x-\sigma(x,z))-2f(x)}{2}(\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)-\nu_{0}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z))\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{f_{\varepsilon}(x+\sigma(x,z))+f_{\varepsilon}(x-\sigma(x,z))-2f_{\varepsilon}(x)}{2}\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right|=:I_{1}(x)+I_{2}(x).

For
I_{1}(x)
, set

G_{x}(z):=\frac{f(x+\sigma(x,z))+f(x-\sigma(x,z))-2f(x)}{2}.

Then by (
3.25
), we have

|G_{x}f(z)|\leqslant\|\nabla^{2}f\|_{\infty}(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}|x|)^{2}|z|^{2},

and

|G_{x}f(z)-G_{x}f(z^{\prime})|\leqslant 2\|\nabla f\|_{\infty}|\sigma(x,z)-\sigma(x,z^{\prime})|.

Thus by (
3.17
) and (
3.6
), we have

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leqslant R}I_{1}(x)\leqslant C_{R}\|f\|_{C^{2}_{b}}\varepsilon^{\beta_{1}}.

For
I_{2}(x)
, noting that by (
3.25
),

|f_{\varepsilon}(x+\sigma(x,z))+f_{\varepsilon}(x-\sigma(x,z))-2f_{\varepsilon}(x)|\leqslant\|\nabla^{2}f\|_{\infty}(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}|x|)^{2}|z|^{2}

and by (
3.20
),

|f_{\varepsilon}(x+\sigma(x,z))+f_{\varepsilon}(x-\sigma(x,z))-2f_{\varepsilon}(x)|\leqslant 4\varepsilon\|\nabla f\|_{\infty}|b(x)|,

we have

\displaystyle I_{2}(x)\leqslant\|\nabla^{2}f\|_{\infty}(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}|x|)^{2}\int_{|z|\leqslant\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}}|z|^{2}\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)+4\varepsilon\|\nabla f\|_{\infty}|b(x)|\int_{|z|>\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}}\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z).

Combining the above calculations and by (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
) and Lemma
3.3
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leqslant R}\big|{\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}f(x)-{\mathcal{A}}^{(0)}f(x)\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{R}\|f\|_{C^{2}_{b}}(\varepsilon^{\beta_{1}}+\varepsilon^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}})\leqslant 2C_{R}\|f\|_{C^{2}_{b}}\varepsilon^{(1-\frac{\alpha}{2})\wedge\beta_{1}},

(3.29)

which together with (
3.24
), (
3.26
) and (
3.27
) yields the desired estimate.
If
b
is bounded and
\kappa_{1}=0
, that is,
|\sigma(t,x,z)|\leqslant\kappa_{0}|z|
, from the above proof, one sees that
C_{R}
is independent of
R
.
∎

For
\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we define

U_{\beta}(x):=(1+|x|^{2})^{\beta/2},\ x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.

We need the following elementary Hölder estimate about
U_{\beta}
.

Lemma 3.10
.

For any
\beta\in(0,2]
, there is a constant
C=C(\beta,d)>0
such that for all
x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

|U_{\beta}(x+y)+U_{\beta}(x-y)-2U_{\beta}(x)|\leqslant C|y|^{\beta}.

Proof.

For
\beta\in(0,1]
, noting that

|U_{\beta}(x+y)-U_{\beta}(x)|\leqslant|g(x+y)-g(x)|^{\beta},

where
g(x):=(1+|x|^{2})^{1/2}
, and by
|\nabla g(x)|\leqslant 1
, we immediately have

\displaystyle|U_{\beta}(x+y)+U_{\beta}(x-y)-2U_{\beta}(x)|

\displaystyle\leqslant|U_{\beta}(x+y)-U_{\beta}(x)|+|U_{\beta}(x-y)-U_{\beta}(x)|

\displaystyle\leqslant|g(x+y)-g(x)|^{\beta}+|g(x-y)-g(x)|^{\beta}\leqslant 2|y|^{\beta}.

For
\beta\in(1,2]
, by Taylor’s expansion we have

U_{\beta}(x+y)+U_{\beta}(x-y)-2U_{\beta}(x)=y\cdot\int^{1}_{0}[\nabla U_{\beta}(x+\theta y)-\nabla U_{\beta}(x-\theta y)]{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta.

In view of
\nabla U_{\beta}(x)=\beta x(1+|x|^{2})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}
, it suffices to show

|x(1+|x|^{2})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}-y(1+|y|^{2})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}|\leqslant C|x-y|^{\beta-1},

furthermore, for each
i=1,\cdots,d
,

|x_{i}(1+|x_{i}|^{2}+|x^{*}_{i}|^{2})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}-y_{i}(1+|y_{i}|^{2}+|y^{*}_{i}|^{2})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}|\leqslant C|x-y|^{\beta-1},

where
x^{*}_{i}
stands for the remaining variables except
x_{i}
.
The above estimate can be derived as a consequence of the following two estimates: for any
a>0
,

|x(a+|x|^{2})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}-y(a+|y|^{2})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}|\leqslant{\big(}\tfrac{\beta}{\beta-1}|x-y|{\big)}^{\beta-1},\ \ x,y\in{\mathbb{R}},

and

|a(1+a^{2}+|x|^{2})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}-a(1+a^{2}+|y|^{2})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}|\leqslant 2|x-y|^{\beta-1},\ \ x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d-1}.

Set

g_{1}(x):=|x|^{\frac{1}{\beta-1}}(a+|x|^{2})^{\frac{\beta-2}{2(\beta-1)}},\ \ g_{2}(x):=a^{\frac{1}{\beta-1}}(1+a^{2}+|x|^{2})^{\frac{\beta-2}{2(\beta-1)}}.

For
\beta\in(1,2]
, it is easy to see that

|g^{\prime}_{1}(x)|\leqslant\tfrac{\beta}{\beta-1},\ \ |\nabla g_{2}(x)|\leqslant 1.

Hence, for
x\cdot y\geqslant 0
,

|x(a+|x|^{2})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}-y(a+|y|^{2})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}|\leqslant|g_{1}(x)-g_{1}(y)|^{\beta-1}\leqslant{\big(}\tfrac{\beta}{\beta-1}|x-y|{\big)}^{\beta-1},

and for
x\cdot y<0
,

|x(a+|x|^{2})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}-y(a+|y|^{2})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}|\leqslant|x|^{\beta-1}+|y|^{\beta-1}\leqslant 2|x-y|^{\beta-1},

and

|a(1+a^{2}+|x|^{2})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}-a(1+a^{2}+|y|^{2})^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}|\leqslant|g_{2}(x)-g_{2}(y)|^{\beta-1}\leqslant|x-y|^{\beta-1}.

The proof is complete.
∎

We need the following technical lemma.

Lemma 3.11
.

Under (
3.18
), for
\kappa_{6}\in{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying

\displaystyle\kappa_{4}+\kappa_{5}<\kappa_{6}\mbox{ if $m=1$ and }\kappa_{6}<0\mbox{ if $m>1$},

(3.30)

there are

\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
and
C_{1}>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-1}\big[\langle x,b_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\rangle+|b_{\varepsilon}(t,x)|^{2}\big]\leqslant\kappa_{6}|x|^{2}+C_{1}.

(3.31)

Proof.

By (
3.19
) and (
3.18
) we have

\varepsilon^{-1}\langle x,b_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\rangle=\frac{\langle x,b(t,x)\rangle}{1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}|b(t,x)|^{1-\frac{1}{m}}}\leqslant\frac{\kappa_{3}+\kappa_{4}|x|^{2}+\kappa_{5}|x|^{m+1}}{1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}|b(t,x)|^{1-\frac{1}{m}}}.

When
m=1
, by
|b_{\varepsilon}(t,x)|\leqslant\varepsilon|b(t,x)|
and (
3.18
), we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-1}\big[\langle x,b_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\rangle+|b_{\varepsilon}(t,x)|^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\kappa_{3}+(\kappa_{4}+\kappa_{5})|x|^{2}}{1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon\kappa_{2}^{2}(1+|x|)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Big(\tfrac{\kappa_{4}+\kappa_{5}}{1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}}+2\varepsilon\kappa^{2}_{2}\Big)|x|^{2}+\tfrac{\kappa_{3}}{1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}}+2\varepsilon\kappa^{2}_{2}.

In particular, for given
\kappa_{6}>\kappa_{4}+\kappa_{5}
, if
\varepsilon_{0}
is small enough, then for some
C_{1}>0
and all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
,

\varepsilon^{-1}\big[\langle x,b_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\rangle+|b_{\varepsilon}(t,x)|^{2}\big]\leqslant\kappa_{6}|x|^{2}+C_{1}.

When
m>1
, for any
K\geqslant 1
, thanks to
\kappa_{5}<0
, by Young’s inequality, there are constants
\varepsilon_{0},C_{0}(K)>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
,

\frac{\kappa_{5}|x|^{m+1}}{1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}|b(t,x)|^{1-\frac{1}{m}}}\leqslant\frac{\kappa_{5}|x|^{m+1}}{1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}(\kappa_{2}(1+|x|))^{m-1}}\leqslant K\kappa_{5}|x|^{2}+C_{0}.

Hence, by
|b_{\varepsilon}(t,x)|^{2}\leqslant\varepsilon|b(t,x)|^{\frac{2}{m}}\leqslant\varepsilon\kappa^{2}_{2}(1+|x|)^{2}
,

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-1}\big[\langle x,b_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\rangle+|b_{\varepsilon}(t,x)|^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\leqslant\kappa_{3}+\kappa_{4}|x|^{2}+K\kappa_{5}|x|^{2}+C_{0}+\kappa^{2}_{2}(1+|x|)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant(\kappa_{4}+2\kappa^{2}_{2}+K\kappa_{5})|x|^{2}+C_{1}(K),

which implies (
3.31
) by
\kappa_{5}<0
and choosing
K
large enough.
∎

Now we show the following Lyapunov’s type estimate.

Lemma 3.12
.

Under (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
) and (
H
{}^{\sigma}_{b}
), for any
\beta\in(0,\alpha)
and
\kappa_{6}\in{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying (
3.30
),
there are constants
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
,
C_{0}=C_{0}(\beta)>0
,
C_{1}=C_{1}(\beta,\nu)>0

and
C_{2}>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
,
s\geqslant 0
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

\displaystyle{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}U_{\beta}(x)\leqslant{\big(}C_{0}\kappa_{6}+C_{1}(\kappa^{2\wedge\alpha}_{1}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\beta\in(0,2)}+\kappa_{1}^{\beta}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\beta\geqslant 2}){\big)}U_{\beta}(x)+C_{2}.

(3.32)

Proof.

It suffices to prove the above estimate for
|x|
being large.
We divide the proofs into three steps. For the sake of simplicity, we drop the time variable.

(
Step 1
). Note that

\nabla U_{\beta}(x)=\beta xU_{\beta-2}(x),

and

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}U_{\beta}(x)=\beta U_{\beta-2}(x){\mathbb{I}}+\beta(\beta-2)U_{\beta-4}(x)(x\otimes x).

(3.33)

By (
3.13
) and (
3.31
), we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}^{(\varepsilon)}U_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-1}\int^{1}_{0}\langle b_{\varepsilon}(x),\nabla U_{\beta}(x+\theta b_{\varepsilon}(x))\rangle{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-1}\beta\int^{1}_{0}\Big[\langle b_{\varepsilon}(x),x\rangle+\theta|b_{\varepsilon}(x)|^{2}\Big]U_{\beta-2}(x+\theta b_{\varepsilon}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta

\displaystyle\leqslant\beta\Big[\kappa_{6}|x|^{2}+C_{1}\Big]\int^{1}_{0}U_{\beta-2}(x+\theta b_{\varepsilon}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta.

We have the following estimate: there is an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
\theta\in(0,1)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
,

\displaystyle(1+|x|^{2})/2\leqslant 1+|x+\theta b_{\varepsilon}(x)|^{2}\leqslant 2(1+|x|^{2}).

(3.34)

In fact, noting that by (
3.20
) and (
3.18
),

\displaystyle|b_{\varepsilon}(x)|\leqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon}|b(x)|^{\frac{1}{m}}\leqslant\sqrt{\varepsilon}\kappa_{2}(1+|x|),

(3.35)

for
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
with
\varepsilon_{0}
small enough, we have

1+|x+\theta b_{\varepsilon}(x)|^{2}\leqslant 1+(|x|+|b_{\varepsilon}(x)|)^{2}\leqslant 1+(|x|+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\kappa_{2}(1+|x|))^{2}\leqslant 2(1+|x|^{2}),

and for
|x|>1
,

1+|x+\theta b_{\varepsilon}(x)|^{2}\geqslant 1+(|x|-|b_{\varepsilon}(x)|)^{2}\geqslant 1+(|x|-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\kappa_{2}(1+|x|))^{2}\geqslant(1+|x|^{2})/2,

and for
|x|\leqslant 1
,

1+|x+\theta b_{\varepsilon}(x)|^{2}\geqslant 1\geqslant(1+|x|^{2})/2.

Hence, we have (
3.34
). Thus, for
\beta\in(0,\alpha)
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}^{(\varepsilon)}U_{\beta}(x)\leqslant\left\{\begin{aligned} &\beta\kappa_{6}|x|^{2}\big(\tfrac{1+|x|^{2}}{2}\big)^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}+C_{1}\big(\tfrac{1+|x|^{2}}{2}\big)^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1},&\kappa_{6}>0,\beta\leqslant 2,\\
&\beta\kappa_{6}|x|^{2}\big(\tfrac{1+|x|^{2}}{2}\big)^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}+C_{1}\big(2(1+|x|^{2})\big)^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1},&\kappa_{6}<0,\beta>2,\\
&\beta\kappa_{6}|x|^{2}\big(2(1+|x|^{2})\big)^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}+C_{1}\big(2(1+|x|^{2})\big)^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1},&\kappa_{6}>0,\beta>2,\\
&\beta\kappa_{6}|x|^{2}\big(2(1+|x|^{2})\big)^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}+C_{1}\big(\tfrac{1+|x|^{2}}{2}\big)^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1},&\kappa_{6}<0,\beta\leqslant 2,\end{aligned}\right.

which implies by Young’s inequality that for some
C_{0}=C_{0}(\beta)>0
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}^{(\varepsilon)}U_{\beta}(x)\leqslant C_{0}\kappa_{6}U_{\beta}(x)+C.

(3.36)

(
Step 2
). In the remaining steps we treat
{\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}U_{\beta}(x)
.
First of all, we consider the case of
\alpha\geqslant 2
and
\beta\in[2,\alpha]
. By (
3.15
),
(
3.25
) and
\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z)=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma(x,z)
, we have

{\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}U_{\beta}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int^{1}_{0}\theta\int^{1}_{-1}\mathrm{tr}((\sigma\otimes\sigma)(x,z)\cdot{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}\nabla^{2}U_{\beta}(x+\theta\theta^{\prime}\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma(x,z))){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta^{\prime}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z).

Since
\beta\geqslant 2
, by (
3.33
) and (
3.35
),
we have for
\varepsilon\leqslant 1/\kappa_{2}^{2}
,

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}U_{\beta}(x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\sigma(x,z)|^{2}\int^{1}_{0}\theta\int^{1}_{-1}U_{\beta-2}(x+\theta\theta^{\prime}\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma(x,z)+b_{\varepsilon}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta^{\prime}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\sigma(x,z)|^{2}(1+|x|^{\beta-2}+|\sigma(x,z)|^{\beta-2}+|b_{\varepsilon}(x)|^{\beta-2})\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\big(}|\sigma(x,z)|^{2}(1+|x|^{\beta-2})+|\sigma(x,z)|^{\beta}{\big)}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z).

By (
3.16
) and (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
), we further have

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}U_{\beta}(x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\big(}(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}|x|)^{2}(1+|x|^{\beta-2})|z|^{2}+(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}|x|)^{\beta}|z|^{\beta}{\big)}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\kappa_{1}^{\beta}|x|^{\beta})\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}(|z|^{2}+|z|^{\beta})\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\lesssim\kappa_{1}^{\beta}U_{\beta}(x)+C.

(
Step 3
). Next we consider the case of
\beta\in(0,2)
.
Let
\kappa_{1}
be the same as in (
3.16
) and write
\gamma:=(4\kappa_{1})^{-1}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2\wedge\alpha}}
.
By (
3.15
) we have

{\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}U_{\beta}(x)=J_{1}(x)+J_{2}(x),

where

J_{1}(x):=\int_{|z|<\gamma}\frac{{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}U_{\beta}(x+\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z))+{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}U_{\beta}(x-\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z))-2{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}U_{\beta}(x)}{2\varepsilon}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

and

J_{2}(x):=\int_{|z|\geqslant\gamma}\frac{{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}U_{\beta}(x+\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z))+{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}U_{\beta}(x-\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z))-2{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}U_{\beta}(x)}{2\varepsilon}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z).

For
J_{1}(x)
, by (
3.25
) and (
3.33
), we have

\displaystyle J_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{|z|<\gamma}\int^{1}_{0}\theta\int^{1}_{-1}\mathrm{tr}{\big(}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}\otimes\sigma_{\varepsilon})(x,z),{\mathcal{D}}^{(\varepsilon)}\nabla^{2}U_{\beta}(x+\theta\theta^{\prime}\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z)){\big)}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta^{\prime}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{|z|<\gamma}\int^{1}_{0}\theta\int^{1}_{-1}\beta|\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z)|^{2}U_{\beta-2}(x+\theta\theta^{\prime}\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z)+b_{\varepsilon}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta^{\prime}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z),

where we have used that for
\beta\in(0,2)
,

\beta(\beta-2)|\langle\sigma(x,z),y\rangle|^{2}U_{\beta-4}(y)\leqslant 0.

For
\varepsilon_{0}
small enough,
and for
|z|<\gamma=(4\kappa_{1})^{-1}\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2\wedge\alpha}}
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and
\theta\in(0,1),\theta^{\prime}\in(-1,1)
,

\displaystyle|x+\theta\theta^{\prime}\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z)+b_{\varepsilon}(x)|

\displaystyle\geqslant|x|-|\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z)|-|b_{\varepsilon}(x)|

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{CB00}}}{{\geqslant}}|x|-(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}|x|)\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2\wedge\alpha}}|z|-|b_{\varepsilon}(x)|

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{BB-1}}}{{\geqslant}}|x|-(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}|x|)(4\kappa_{1})^{-1}-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\kappa_{2}(1+|x|)

\displaystyle\geqslant|x|/2-C_{3}.

Thus for
|x|>4C_{3}
, by (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
),

\displaystyle J_{1}(x)

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{|z|<\gamma}\frac{\beta|\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z)|^{2}}{2\varepsilon}\Big(1+\big|\tfrac{|x|}{2}-C_{3}\big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{\beta-2}{2}}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{|z|<\gamma}\frac{\beta(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}|x|)^{2}\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{2\wedge\alpha}}|z|^{2}}{2\varepsilon}\Big(\tfrac{|x|^{2}}{16}\Big)^{\frac{\beta-2}{2}}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\beta(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}|x|)^{2}\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{2\wedge\alpha}}}{2\varepsilon}\Big(\tfrac{|x|^{2}}{16}\Big)^{\frac{\beta-2}{2}}\gamma^{2-(2\wedge\alpha)}\leqslant C_{1}\kappa_{1}^{2\wedge\alpha}|x|^{\beta}+C_{2}.

For
J_{2}(x)
, since
\beta\in(0,2)
, by Lemma
3.10
, (
H
{}^{\sigma}_{b}
) and Lemma
3.3
, we directly have

\displaystyle J_{2}(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{|z|\geqslant\gamma}|\sigma_{\varepsilon}(x,z)|^{\beta}\varepsilon^{-1}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle\leqslant(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}|x|)^{\beta}\varepsilon^{\frac{\beta}{2-2\wedge\alpha}-1}\int_{|z|\geqslant\gamma}|z|^{\beta}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle\lesssim(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}|x|)^{\beta}\varepsilon^{\frac{\beta}{2-2\wedge\alpha}-1}\gamma^{\beta-2\wedge\alpha}\leqslant C_{1}\kappa_{1}^{2\wedge\alpha}|x|^{\beta}+C_{2}.

Hence, for
|x|\geqslant 4C_{3}
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}^{(\varepsilon)}U_{\beta}(x)\leqslant C_{1}\kappa_{1}^{2\wedge\alpha}U_{\beta}(x)+C_{2}.

(3.37)

The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 3.13
.

From the above proofs, one sees that
if
|\sigma(t,x,z)|\leqslant\kappa_{0}|z|
, then for any
\beta\in(0,\alpha)
,

{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}U_{\beta}(x)\leqslant C_{0}\kappa_{6}U_{\beta}(x)+C_{2},

where
\kappa_{6}
is given in (
3.30
).

As an easy corollary, we have

Corollary 3.14
.

Under (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
) and (
H
{}^{\sigma}_{b}
),
for any
\beta\in(0,\alpha)
and
T>0
, it holds that for some
C_{1}>0
depending on
T
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|X^{\varepsilon}_{t}|^{\beta}\right)\leqslant C_{1}(1+{\mathbb{E}}|X_{0}|^{\beta}),

(3.38)

and for some
C_{2}>0
independent of
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
t>0
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}U_{\beta}(X^{\varepsilon}_{t})\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{\kappa_{7}t}{\mathbb{E}}U_{\beta}(X_{0})+C_{2}(\mathrm{e}^{\kappa_{7}t}-1)/\kappa_{7},

(3.39)

where
\kappa_{7}:=C_{0}\kappa_{6}+C_{1}(\kappa^{2\wedge\alpha}_{1}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\beta\in(0,2)}+\kappa_{1}^{\beta}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\beta\geqslant 2})\in{\mathbb{R}}
(see Lemma
3.12
).

Proof.

By Itô’s formula and Lemma
3.12
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa_{7}t}U_{\beta}(X^{\varepsilon}_{t})

\displaystyle=U_{\beta}(X_{0})+\int^{t}_{0}\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa_{7}s}({\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}U_{\beta}-\kappa_{7}U_{\beta})(X^{\varepsilon}_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+M^{\varepsilon}_{t}

\displaystyle\leqslant U_{\beta}(X_{0})+C_{2}\int^{t}_{0}\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa_{7}s}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+M^{\varepsilon}_{t},

(3.40)

where
M^{\varepsilon}_{t}
is a local martingale given by

M^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\int^{t}_{0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa_{7}s}\left(U_{\beta}{\big(}X^{\varepsilon}_{s-}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-},z)+b_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-}){\big)}-U_{\beta}(X^{\varepsilon}_{s-})\right)\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z).

By applying stochastic Gronwall’s lemma (see
[
41
, Lemma 3.7]
) and utilizing the fact that
\beta
can be chosen arbitrarily in the interval
(0,\alpha)
, we obtain equation (
3.38
).
Moreover, for
R>0
, define

\tau^{\varepsilon}_{R}:=\inf\big\{t>0:|X^{\varepsilon}_{t}|\geqslant R\big\}.

By the optimal stopping theorem and taking expectations for (
3.40
), we also have

{\mathbb{E}}\Big(\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa_{7}t\wedge\tau^{\varepsilon}_{R}}U_{\beta}(X^{\varepsilon}_{t\wedge\tau^{\varepsilon}_{R}})\Big)\leqslant{\mathbb{E}}U_{\beta}(X_{0})+C_{2}\big(1-{\mathbb{E}}\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa_{7}t\wedge\tau^{\varepsilon}_{R}}\big)/\kappa_{7}.

Letting
R\to\infty
and by Fatou’s lemma, we obtain (
3.39
).
∎

For given
T>0
, let
{\mathscr{T}}_{T}
be the set of all stopping times bounded by
T
.

Lemma 3.15
.

For any
T,\gamma>0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\sup_{\tau,\eta\in{\mathscr{T}}_{T},\tau\leqslant\eta\leqslant\tau+\delta}{\mathbb{P}}\left(|X^{\varepsilon}_{\eta}-X^{\varepsilon}_{\tau}|>\gamma\right)=0.

Proof.

Let
\tau,\eta\in{\mathscr{T}}_{T}
with
\tau\leqslant\eta\leqslant\tau+\delta
. For any
R>0
, define

\zeta_{R}:=\inf\big\{t>0:|X^{\varepsilon}_{t}|>R\big\},\ \tau_{R}:=\zeta_{R}\wedge\tau,\ \eta_{R}:=\zeta_{R}\wedge\eta.

We prove the limit for
\alpha\in(0,2)
. For
\alpha=2
, it is easier.
By (
3.9
), we can write

\displaystyle X^{\varepsilon}_{\eta_{R}}-X^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{R}}

\displaystyle=\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}b_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{s}+\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{|z|<\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-},z){\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle\quad+\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{|z|>\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-},z){\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=:I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}.

For
I_{1}
, by (
3.20
) and (
3.18
), we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|I_{1}|\leqslant\varepsilon{\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}|b(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-})|{\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{s}\right)={\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}|b(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s})|{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)\leqslant C_{R}\delta.

For
I_{2}
, by (
3.10
) and the isometry of stochastic integrals, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|I_{2}|^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left|\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{|z|<\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s-},z)\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{|z|<\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}}|\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s},z)|^{2}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\big(}\tfrac{s}{\varepsilon}{\big)}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{|z|<1}|\sigma(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s},z)|^{2}\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}R)^{2}\left(\int_{|z|<1}|z|^{2}\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right)\delta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{VV9}}}{{\leqslant}}C_{R}\delta.

Fix
\beta\in(0,\alpha\wedge 1)
. For
I_{3}
, by
|\sum_{i}a_{i}|^{\beta}\leqslant\sum_{i}a_{i}^{\beta}
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|I_{3}|^{\beta}

\displaystyle\leqslant{\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{|z|\geqslant\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}}|\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s},z)|^{\beta}{\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{|z|\geqslant\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}}|\sigma_{\varepsilon}(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s},z)|^{\beta}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\big(}\tfrac{s}{\varepsilon}{\big)}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{|z|\geqslant 1}|\sigma(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s},z)|^{\beta}\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}R)^{\beta}\left(\int_{|z|\geqslant 1}|z|^{\beta}\nu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right)\delta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{VV9}}}{{\leqslant}}C_{R}\delta.

Hence, by Chebyshev’s inequality and (
3.38
),

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(|X^{\varepsilon}_{\eta}-X^{\varepsilon}_{\tau}|\geqslant\gamma)

\displaystyle\leqslant{\mathbb{P}}(|X^{\varepsilon}_{\eta_{R}}-X^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{R}}|\geqslant\gamma;\zeta_{R}>T)+{\mathbb{P}}(\zeta_{R}\leqslant T)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{3}{\mathbb{P}}(|I_{i}|\geqslant\tfrac{\gamma}{3})+{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|X^{\varepsilon}_{t}|\geqslant R\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\tfrac{3}{\gamma}{\mathbb{E}}|I_{1}|+(\tfrac{3}{\gamma})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}|I_{2}|^{2}+(\tfrac{3}{\gamma})^{\beta}{\mathbb{E}}|I_{3}|^{\beta}+\tfrac{C}{R^{\beta}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{R,\gamma}\delta+C/R^{\beta},

which converges to zero by firstly letting
\delta\to 0
and then
R\to\infty
.
∎

Let
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\varepsilon}
be the law of
(X^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
in
{\mathbb{D}}
.
Now we can show the following main result of this section.

Theorem 3.16
.

Let
\mu_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
be the law of
X^{\varepsilon}_{0}
.
Suppose that (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
) and (
H
{}^{\sigma}_{b}
) hold, and
\mu_{\varepsilon}
weakly converges to
\mu_{0}
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
,
and there is a unique martingale solution
{\mathbb{Q}}
associated with
{\mathscr{L}}^{(0)}
starting from
\mu_{0}
at time
0
.
Then
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\varepsilon}
weakly converges to
{\mathbb{Q}}
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
. Moreover, if
\alpha\geqslant 2
, then
{\mathbb{Q}}_{0}
concentrates on the space of all continuous functions.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.15
and Aldous’ criterion (see
[
23
, p356, Theorem 4.5]
),
({\mathbb{Q}}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}
is tight in
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}})
.
Let
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
be any accumulation point. By Lemma
3.9
and Theorem
6.4
in appendix, one has
{\mathbb{Q}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\mu_{0}}_{0}({\mathscr{L}}^{(0)})
.
By the uniqueness, we have
{\mathbb{Q}}_{0}={\mathbb{Q}}
and
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\varepsilon}
weakly converges to
{\mathbb{Q}}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
If
\alpha\geqslant 2
, then by Proposition
6.3
,
{\mathbb{Q}}
concentrates on the space of all continuous functions.
∎

3.2.
Convergence of invariant measures

In this section we show the following convergence of invariant measures under dissipativity assumptions.

Theorem 3.17
.

Suppose that
b
and
\sigma
do not depend on the time variable. Under (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
) and (
H
{}^{\sigma}_{b}
), if for some
\beta\in(0,\alpha)
,

\kappa_{7}(\beta):=C_{0}\kappa_{6}+C_{1}(\kappa^{2\wedge\alpha}_{1}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\beta\in(0,2)}+\kappa_{1}^{\beta}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\beta\geqslant 2})<0,

where the above constants appear in Lemma
3.12
, then for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
there is an invariant probability measure
\mu_{\varepsilon}
associated with the semigroup
P^{\varepsilon}_{t}f(x):={\mathbb{E}}f(X^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x))
, where
X^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x)
is the unique solution of SDE (
3.9
) starting from
X^{\varepsilon}_{0}=x
.
Moreover,
(\mu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\subset{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
is tight and any accumulation point
\mu_{0}
is a stationary distribution of SDE (
3.22
).

Proof.

Let
\beta\in(0,\alpha)
. If
\kappa_{7}(\beta)<0
, then by (
3.39
), it is easy to see that

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon}\sup_{T\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{T}\int^{T}_{0}{\mathbb{E}}|X^{\varepsilon}_{t}|^{\beta}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}t<\infty.

(3.41)

For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
T\geqslant 1
, we define a probability measure over
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
by

\mu_{\varepsilon,T}(A):=\frac{1}{T}\int^{T}_{0}{\mathbb{P}}\{X^{\varepsilon}_{t}\in A\}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}t,\ \ A\in{\mathscr{B}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}).

By (
3.41
), one sees that
(\mu_{\varepsilon,T})_{T\geqslant 1}
is tight. Let
\mu_{\varepsilon}
be any accumulation point of
(\mu_{\varepsilon,T})_{T\geqslant 1}
.
By the classical Krylov-Bogoliubov argument (cf.
[
10
, Section 3.1]
), one can verify that
\mu_{\varepsilon}
is an invariant probability measure
associated with the semigroup
(P^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
, and by (
3.41
),

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|x|^{\beta}\mu_{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x)<\infty.

From this, by Prohorov’s theorem we derive that
(\mu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}
is tight.
Let
\mu_{0}
be any accumulation point of
(\mu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}
and for subsequence
\varepsilon_{k}\downarrow 0
,

\mu_{\varepsilon_{k}}
weakly converges to
\mu_{0}
as
k\to\infty
.
Let
X^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{0}

have the distribution
\mu_{\varepsilon_{k}}
and
X^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{t}
be the unique solution of SDE (
3.9
).
Since
\mu_{\varepsilon_{k}}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
is an invariant probability measure of SDE (
3.9
),
we have for each
t>0
and
f\in C_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,

\mu_{\varepsilon_{k}}(f)={\mathbb{E}}f(X^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{t}).

By Theorem
3.16
and taking weak limits, we obtain

\mu_{0}(f)={\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{Q}}}f(w_{t}),\ \ t>0,

where
{\mathbb{Q}}
is a martingale solution of SDE (
3.22
) with initial distribution
\mu_{0}
. In other words,
\mu_{0}
is a stationary distribution of
{\mathbb{Q}}
.
∎

Remark 3.18
.

If SDE (
3.22
) has a unique stationary distribution
\mu
(or invariant probability measure),
then
\mu_{\varepsilon}\Rightarrow\mu
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.

Example.
Let
\alpha\in(0,2]
and consider the following SDE

\displaystyle{\mathord{{\rm d}}}X_{t}=\sigma(X_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}L^{(\alpha)}_{t}+b(X_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t,\ X_{0}=x,

(3.42)

where for
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
L^{(\alpha)}_{t}
is a standard rotationally invariant and symmetric
\alpha
-stable process, and for
\alpha=2
,

L^{(2)}_{t}
is a
d
-dimensional standard Brownian motion,
\sigma:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\otimes{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and

b:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
are two locally Lipschitz continuous functions. Suppose that for some
\kappa_{1}\geqslant\kappa_{0}>0
,

\kappa_{0}|\xi|^{2}\leqslant|\sigma(x)\xi|^{2}\leqslant\kappa_{1}|\xi|^{2},

and for some
m\geqslant 1
and
\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4}>0
and
\kappa_{5}<0
(with
\kappa_{4}+\kappa_{5}<0
in the case of
m=1
),

|b(x)|\leqslant(\kappa_{2}(1+|x|))^{m},\ \ \langle x,b(x)\rangle\leqslant\kappa_{3}+\kappa_{4}|x|^{2}+\kappa_{5}|x|^{m+1}.

It is well-known that SDE (
3.42
) has a unique invariant probability measure
\mu
(see
[
42
]
).
If we consider the approximating SDE (
3.9
) with

\sigma_{\varepsilon}
and
b_{\varepsilon}
being defined by (
3.19
), then SDE (
3.9
) admits an invariant probability measure
\mu_{\varepsilon}
, and by Theorem
3.17
,

\mu_{\varepsilon}\Rightarrow\mu,\ \ \varepsilon\downarrow 0.

3.3.
Rate of weak convergence

Now we aim to show the rate of weak convergence as done for ODE (see Theorem
2.1
). However, in this case, we will utilize the regularity estimate for the associated parabolic equation. To achieve this, we will require the following stronger assumptions:

(
H
′
)

Suppose that for some
\kappa_{1}>0
and all
0\not=z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

\|b\|_{\infty}+\|\nabla b\|_{\infty}+\|\sigma(\cdot,z)/|z|\|_{\infty}+\|\nabla_{z}\sigma\|_{\infty}+\|\nabla_{x}\sigma(\cdot,z)/|z|\|_{\infty}\leqslant\kappa_{1},

and for any
\varphi\in C^{1}_{b}
and
t>0
, the following parabolic equation admits a solution
u
,

\partial_{s}u+{\mathscr{L}}^{(0)}_{s}u=0,\ s\in[0,t),\ u(t,x)=\varphi(x),

with regularity estimate that for some
\gamma>2
and
\beta<1
,

\displaystyle\|u(s,\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leqslant\|\varphi\|_{\infty},\ \ \|u(s,\cdot)\|_{C^{\gamma}_{b}}\leqslant C(t-s)^{-\beta}\|\varphi\|_{C^{1}_{b}},\ \ s\in[0,t).

(3.43)

We can show

Theorem 3.19
.

Under (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
) and
(H
′
)
, for any
\varphi\in C^{1}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
T>0
, there is a constant
C>0
such that for all
t\in[0,T]
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle|{\mathbb{E}}\varphi(X^{\varepsilon}_{t})-{\mathbb{E}}\varphi(X_{t})|\leqslant C\Big(\varepsilon^{\frac{(\alpha-2)\wedge 1}{2}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha\in(2,\gamma]}+\varepsilon^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}\wedge\beta_{1}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha<2}\Big)\|\varphi\|_{C^{1}_{b}},

(3.44)

where
\beta_{1}
is from (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
) and
\gamma
is from
(H
′
)
.

Proof.

Fix
t>0
.
Under
(H
′
)
, by Itô’s formula, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\varphi(X^{\varepsilon}_{t})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}u(t,X^{\varepsilon}_{t})={\mathbb{E}}u(0,X_{0})+{\mathbb{E}}\int^{t}_{0}(\partial_{s}u+{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}u)(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

and

{\mathbb{E}}\varphi(X_{t})={\mathbb{E}}u(t,X_{t})={\mathbb{E}}u(0,X_{0}).

Hence, by Lemma
3.15
,

\displaystyle|{\mathbb{E}}\varphi(X^{\varepsilon}_{t})-{\mathbb{E}}\varphi(X_{t})|

\displaystyle=\left|{\mathbb{E}}\int^{t}_{0}({\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}u-{\mathscr{L}}^{(0)}_{s}u)(s,X^{\varepsilon}_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right|\leqslant\int^{t}_{0}\|{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}u(s)-{\mathscr{L}}^{(0)}_{s}u(s)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\int^{t}_{0}\Big(\varepsilon^{\frac{(\alpha-2)\wedge 1}{2}}\|u(s)\|_{C^{\alpha}_{b}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha\in(2,\gamma]}+\varepsilon^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}\wedge\beta_{1}}\|u(s)\|_{C^{2}_{b}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha<2}\Big){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big(\varepsilon^{\frac{(\alpha-2)\wedge 1}{2}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha\in(2,\gamma]}+\varepsilon^{\frac{2-\alpha}{2}\wedge\beta_{1}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha<2}\Big)\int^{t}_{0}(t-s)^{-\beta}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,

which yields the desired estimate by
\beta<1
.
∎

Remark 3.20
.

Estimate (
3.43
) is the classical Schauder estimate, which is well-studied in the literature of partial differential equations (PDEs), particularly
for the case of continuous diffusion with
\alpha=2
. In the case of
\alpha\in(1,2)
, the estimate can be found in
[
17
]
. Here, we provide a brief proof specifically for the additive noise case. We consider the following forward PDE:

\partial_{t}u=\Delta^{\alpha/2}u+b\cdot\nabla u,\ \ u(0)=\varphi,\ \ \alpha\in(1,2].

Let
P_{t}
be the semigroup associated with
\Delta^{\alpha/2}
, that is,

P_{t}\varphi(x)={\mathbb{E}}\varphi(x+L^{(\alpha)}_{t}).

By Duhamel’s formula, we have

u(t,x)=P_{t}\varphi(x)+\int^{t}_{0}P_{t-s}(b\cdot\nabla u)(s,x){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s.

It is well-known that by the gradient estimate of heat kernels, for
\beta,\gamma\geqslant 0
(see
[
7
]

[
17
]
),

\|P_{t}\varphi\|_{C^{\beta+\gamma}_{b}}\leqslant Ct^{-\frac{\beta}{\alpha}}\|\varphi\|_{C^{\gamma}_{b}},\ \ t>0.

Hence, for
\beta\in(2-\alpha,1]
and
\gamma\in(2,\alpha+\beta)
,

\displaystyle\|u(t)\|_{C^{\gamma}_{b}}

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{-\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}\|\varphi\|_{C^{1}_{b}}+\int^{t}_{0}(t-s)^{\frac{\beta-\gamma}{\alpha}}\|b(s)\cdot\nabla u(s)\|_{C^{\beta}_{b}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{-\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}\|\varphi\|_{C^{1}_{b}}+\int^{t}_{0}(t-s)^{\frac{\beta-\gamma}{\alpha}}\|b(s)\|_{C^{\beta}_{b}}\|u(s)\|_{C^{\beta+1}_{b}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s.

By Gronwall’s inequality of Volterra’s type, we obtain that for any
\gamma\in(2,\alpha+\beta)
,

\|u(t)\|_{C^{\gamma}_{b}}\lesssim t^{-\frac{\gamma-1}{\alpha}}\|\varphi\|_{C^{1}_{b}}.

In this case we have the weak convergence rate (
3.44
) for Hölder drift
b
.

4.
Compound Poisson approximation for 2D-NSEs

In this section, we develop a discrete compound Poisson approximation for the 2D Navier-Stokes or Euler equations on the torus. We shall show the optimal rate of convergence for this approximation.
Our scheme heavily relies on the stochastic Lagrangian particle representation of the NSEs, which has been previously studied in works such as
[
30
]
,
[
8
]
, and
[
43
]
.

4.1.
Diffeomorphism flow of SDEs driven by compound Poisson processes

In this subsection we show the diffeomorphism flow property of SDEs driven by compound Poisson processes and the connection with difference equations.
More precisely, fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and let
X_{s,t}(x)
solve the following SDE:

\displaystyle X_{s,t}(x)

\displaystyle=x+\int^{t}_{s}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\Big(b_{\varepsilon}(r,X_{s,r-}(x))+\sqrt{\varepsilon}z\Big){\mathcal{H}}^{\varepsilon}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}r,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z),\ \ t>s\geqslant 0,

where
b_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded continuous function, and
\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}
is defined as in (
3.2
). By the definition, we can rephrase the above SDE as follows:

\displaystyle X_{s,t}(x)

\displaystyle=x+\sum_{r\in(s,t]}\Big(b_{\varepsilon}(r,X_{s,r-}(x))+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\Delta H_{r}\Big){\mathbbm{1}}_{\Delta{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{r}=1}

\displaystyle=x+\int^{t}_{s}b_{\varepsilon}(r,X_{s,r-}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{r}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}(H^{\varepsilon}_{t}-H^{\varepsilon}_{s}),

(4.1)

where
{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{r}
is defined by (
2.1
) and
H^{\varepsilon}_{t}
is defined by (
3.1
).
For given
T>0
, bounded continuous functions
\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
f:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, define

u(s,x):={\mathbb{E}}\varphi(X_{s,T}(x))+\int^{T}_{s}{\mathbb{E}}f(r,X_{s,r}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r,\ s\in[0,T].

Since
s\mapsto{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{s}
is stochastically continuous and
b_{\varepsilon}
is bi-continuous,
by (
4.1
) and the dominated convergence theorem, it is easy to see that

(s,x)\mapsto u(s,x)
is bi-continuous on
[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

(4.2)

The following lemma states that
u
solves the backward Kolmogorov equation. Although the proof is standard, we provide a detailed proof for the convenience of the readers.

Lemma 4.1
.

For each
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, the function
s\mapsto u(s,x)
is continuous differentiable, and

\displaystyle\partial_{s}u(s,x)+{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}u(s,x)+f(s,x)=0,\ \ s\in[0,T],

(4.3)

where

{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}\varphi(x):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{\varphi(x+\sqrt{\varepsilon}z+b_{\varepsilon}(s,x))-\varphi(x)}{\varepsilon}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z).

Proof.

Fix
s,h\in[0,T]
with
s+h\leqslant T
. Note that by the flow property of
X_{s,t}(x)
,

X_{s,T}(x)=X_{s+h,T}\circ X_{s,s+h}(x).

This follows directly from the unique solvability of SDE (
4.1
). Since
X_{s+h,T}(\cdot)
and
X_{s,s+h}(\cdot)
are independent, by definition we have

\displaystyle u(s,x)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\varphi(X_{s+h,T}\circ X_{s,s+h}(x))+\int^{T}_{s+h}{\mathbb{E}}f(r,X_{s+h,r}\circ X_{s,s+h}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r+\int^{s+h}_{s}{\mathbb{E}}f(r,X_{s,r}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathbb{E}}\varphi(X_{s+h,T}(y))+\int^{T}_{s+h}{\mathbb{E}}f(r,X_{s+h,r}(y)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right]_{y=X_{s,s+h}(x)}+\int^{s+h}_{s}{\mathbb{E}}f(r,X_{s,r}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}u(s+h,X_{s,s+h}(x))+\int^{s+h}_{s}{\mathbb{E}}f(r,X_{s,r}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r.

Applying Itô’s formula to
u(s+h,\cdot)
, we have

{\mathbb{E}}u(s+h,X_{s,s+h}(x))=u(s+h,x)+\int^{s+h}_{s}{\mathbb{E}}{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{r}u(s+h,X_{s,r}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r.

Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{u(s+h,x)-u(s,x)}{h}=-\frac{1}{h}\int^{s+h}_{s}\Big({\mathbb{E}}{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{r}u(s+h,X_{s,r}(x))+{\mathbb{E}}f(r,X_{s,r}(x))\Big){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r.

By the dominated convergence theorem and (
4.2
), it is easy to see that

\partial^{+}_{s}u(s,x)+{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}u(s,x)+f(s,x)=0,

where
\partial^{+}_{s}
(resp.
\partial^{-}_{s}
) stands for the right (resp. left) hand derivative. Similarly, we can show

\partial^{-}_{s}u(s,x)+{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}u(s,x)+f(s,x)=0.

Since
(s,x)\mapsto{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}u(s,x)+f(s,x)
is continuous, we complete the proof.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

The continuity of
b_{\varepsilon}
and
f
in time variable
t
can be dropped by smooth approximation. In this case, (
4.3
) holds only for Lebesgue almost all
s\in[0,T]
.

Next, we investigate the
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism property of the mapping
x\mapsto X_{s,t}(x)
.
To ensure the homeomorphism property of this mapping, we need to impose a condition on the gradient of
b_{\varepsilon}(s,x)
. More specifically, we assume that the gradient of
b_{\varepsilon}(s,x)
is not too large.

Theorem 4.3
.

Suppose that
(s,x)\mapsto\nabla b_{\varepsilon}(s,x)
is continuous and for some
\kappa>0
,

\displaystyle|\nabla_{x}b_{\varepsilon}(s,x)|\leqslant\kappa\varepsilon\leqslant 1,\ \ \mathord{{\rm div}}b_{\varepsilon}=0.

(4.4)

Then there is an
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
,

\{X_{s,t}(x),x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\}_{0\leqslant s<t}
forms a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism flow and for some constant
C=C(d)>0
,

{\mathbb{E}}\det(\nabla X_{s,t}(x))+{\mathbb{E}}\det(\nabla X_{s,t}(x))^{-1}\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{C\kappa^{2}\varepsilon(t-s)}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
s=0
and write
X_{t}:=X_{0,t}(x)
.
Let
J_{t}:=\nabla X_{t}
. By (
4.1
) we clearly have

J_{t}={\mathbb{I}}+\int^{t}_{0}\nabla b_{\varepsilon}(s,X_{s-})J_{s-}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{s},

and by Itô’s formula,

\displaystyle\det(J_{t})

\displaystyle=1+\int^{t}_{0}\Big[\det(({\mathbb{I}}+\nabla b_{\varepsilon}(s,X_{s-}))J_{s-})-\det(J_{s-})\Big]{\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{s}

\displaystyle=1+\int^{t}_{0}\Big[\det({\mathbb{I}}+\nabla b_{\varepsilon}(s,X_{s-}))-1\Big]\det(J_{s-}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{s}.

(4.5)

Note that for a matrix
B=(b_{ij})
with
|b_{ij}|\leqslant\ell
(see
[
45
, Lemma 2.1]
),

|\det({\mathbb{I}}+B)-1-\mathrm{tr}B|\leqslant C_{d}\ell^{2}(1+\ell)^{d-2}.

By (
4.4
) we have

\big|\det({\mathbb{I}}+\nabla b_{\varepsilon}(s,X_{s-}))-1\big|\leqslant C\kappa^{2}\varepsilon^{2},

and there is an
\varepsilon_{0}
small enough so that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
,

\big|\det({\mathbb{I}}+\nabla b_{\varepsilon}(s,X_{s-}))^{-1}-1\big|\leqslant C\kappa^{2}\varepsilon^{2}.

Thus by (
4.5
), we have

\det(J_{t})^{-1}=1+\int^{t}_{0}\Big[\det({\mathbb{I}}+\nabla b_{\varepsilon}(s,X_{s-}))^{-1}-1\Big]\det(J_{s-})^{-1}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{s}.

Hence,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\det(J_{t})=1+{\mathbb{E}}\int^{t}_{0}\Big[\det({\mathbb{I}}+\nabla b_{\varepsilon}(s,X_{s}))-1\Big]\det(J_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\big(}\frac{s}{\varepsilon}{\big)}\leqslant 1+C\kappa^{2}\varepsilon\int^{t}_{0}{\mathbb{E}}\det(J_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,

and also

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\det(J_{t})^{-1}\leqslant 1+C\kappa^{2}\varepsilon\int^{t}_{0}{\mathbb{E}}\det(J_{s})^{-1}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s.

By Gronwall’s inequality, we obtain the desired estimates.
∎

4.2.
Compound Poisson approximation for 2D-NSEs

Fix
T>0
. In this subsection we consider the following backward 2D-NSE on the torus
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}=[-\pi,\pi]^{2}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{s}u+\nu\Delta u+u\cdot\nabla u+\nabla p=0,\ \ \mathord{{\rm div}}u=0,\ \ u(T)=\varphi,

(4.6)

where
\nu
stands for the viscosity constant and
p
is the pressure,
\varphi:{\mathbb{T}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is a divergence free smooth velocity field.
Let
w={\rm curl}(u)
be the curl of
u
. Then
w
solves the following vorticity equation

\displaystyle\partial_{s}w+\nu\Delta w+u\cdot\nabla w=0,\ \ \ w(T)={\rm curl}(\varphi)=:w_{0}.

(4.7)

If we assume

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{2}}u(x){\mathord{{\rm d}}}x=0,

then the velocity field
u
can be uniquely recovered from vorticity
w
by the Biot-Savart law:

u=K_{2}*w,

where
K_{2}
is the Biot-Savart kernel on the torus and takes the following form (see
[
30
, (2.19)]
and
[
37
, p256, Theorem 2.17]
):

\displaystyle K_{2}(x):=(-x_{2},x_{1})/(2\pi|x|^{2})+K_{0}(x),\ \ \ K_{0}\in C^{\infty}([-\pi,\pi]^{2}).

(4.8)

Since
K_{2}\in L^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{2})
, we clearly have

\displaystyle\|K_{2}*w\|_{\infty}\leqslant C\|w\|_{\infty}.

(4.9)

Let
X_{s,t}(x)
solve the following nonlinear SDE on the torus
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} X_{s,t}(x)&=x+\int^{t}_{s}u(r,X_{s,r}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+\sqrt{\nu}W_{t},\ t\in[s,T],\\
w(s,x)&={\mathbb{E}}w_{0}(X_{s,T}(x)),\ \ u=K_{2}*w.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.10)

It is well-known that there is a one-to-one correspondence between (
4.6
) and (
4.7
) (see
[
30
]

[
8
]

[
43
]
).
Motivated by the approximation in Section 3, we may construct the compound Poisson approximation for system (
4.10
) as follows:
for
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} X^{\varepsilon}_{s,t}(x)&=x+\varepsilon\int^{t}_{s}u_{\varepsilon}(r,X^{\varepsilon}_{s,r-}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{r}+\sqrt{\varepsilon\nu}(H^{\varepsilon}_{t}-H^{\varepsilon}_{s}),\\
