w_{\varepsilon}(s,x)&={\mathbb{E}}w_{0}(X^{\varepsilon}_{s,T}(x)),\ \ u_{\varepsilon}=K_{2}*w_{\varepsilon},\end{aligned}\right.

(4.11)

where
H^{\varepsilon}_{t}
is a compound Poisson process defined in (
1.13
). By Lemma
4.1
,

w_{\varepsilon}
solves the following nonlinear discrete difference equation:

\partial_{s}w_{\varepsilon}+{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}w_{\varepsilon}=0,\ \ u_{\varepsilon}=K_{2}*w_{\varepsilon},

where

{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x):=\sum_{i=1,2}\frac{f(x+\sqrt{\varepsilon\nu}e_{i}+\varepsilon u_{\varepsilon}(s,x))+f(x-\sqrt{\varepsilon\nu}e_{i}+\varepsilon u_{\varepsilon}(s,x))-2f(x)}{2\varepsilon}.

The following Beale-Kato-Majda’s estimate for the Biot-Savart law on the torus is crucial for solving stochastic system (
4.11
).

Lemma 4.4
.

For any
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
there is a constant
C=C(\gamma)>0
such that for any
w\in C^{\gamma}_{b}({\mathbb{T}}^{2})
,

\|\nabla(K_{2}*w)\|_{\infty}\leqslant C\big(1+\|w\|_{\infty}(1+\log(1+[w]_{\gamma}))\big),

where
[w]_{\gamma}:=\sup_{x\not=y}\frac{|w(x)-w(y)|}{|x-y|^{\gamma}}
.

Proof.

Let
H(x):=(-x_{2},x_{1})/(2\pi|x|^{2})
. By (
4.8
),
it suffices to make an estimate for
\nabla H*w
.
For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, by definition and the cancellation property
\int_{|y|=s}\nabla H(y){\mathord{{\rm d}}}y=0
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla H*w(x)={\rm p.v.}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{2}}\nabla H(y)w(x-y){\mathord{{\rm d}}}y=I_{\varepsilon}(x)+J_{\varepsilon}(x),

where

\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle:=\int_{|y|\leqslant\varepsilon}\nabla H(y)(w(x-y)-w(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}y,

\displaystyle J_{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\varepsilon<|y|\leqslant\pi}\nabla H(y)w(x-y){\mathord{{\rm d}}}y.

For
I_{\varepsilon}
, since
|\nabla H(y)|\leqslant 4|y|^{-2}
, we have

\|I_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\leqslant 4[w]_{\gamma}\int_{|y|\leqslant\varepsilon}|y|^{\gamma-2}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}y\leqslant C[w]_{\gamma}\varepsilon^{\gamma}.

For
J_{\varepsilon}
, we have

\|J_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\leqslant 4\|w\|_{\infty}\int_{\varepsilon<|y|\leqslant\pi}|y|^{-2}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}y\leqslant C\|w\|_{\infty}(1+\log 1/\varepsilon).

Combining the above two estimates and choosing
\varepsilon=([w]_{\gamma}+1)^{-1}
, we obtain

\|H*w\|_{\infty}\leqslant C\big(1+\|w\|_{\infty}(1+\log(1+[w]_{\gamma}))\big).

The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

In the whole space, the above estimates need to be modified as follows (see
[
30
]
):

\|\nabla u\|_{\infty}\leqslant C\big(1+\|w\|_{\infty}(1+\log(1+[w]_{\gamma}+\|w\|_{p}))\big),\ \ p\in[1,\infty).

The presence of
\|w\|_{p}
and the Jacobian determinant in Theorem
4.3
, which depend on the bound of
\nabla b_{\varepsilon}
, pose challenges when solving the approximating equation (
4.11
) for NSEs on the entire space.
This is why we consider NSEs on the torus instead.

Now we can establish the solvability for stochastic system (
4.11
).

Theorem 4.6
.

For any
w_{0}\in C^{1}_{b}({\mathbb{T}}^{2})
, there is a unique solution
X^{\varepsilon}_{s,t}(x)
to stochastic system (
4.11
) so that
w_{\varepsilon}\in C([0,T];C^{1}_{b}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}))
and there is a constant
C>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
s\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla w_{\varepsilon}(s)\|_{\infty}\leqslant C.

(4.12)

Proof.

We use Picard’s iteration method. Let
u_{0}(t,x)=K_{2}*w_{0}(x)
. For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
X^{n}_{s,t}(x)
solve

\displaystyle X^{n}_{s,t}(x)=x+\varepsilon\int^{t}_{s}u_{n-1}(r,X^{n}_{s,r-}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{r}+\sqrt{\varepsilon\nu}(H^{\varepsilon}_{t}-H^{\varepsilon}_{s}),\ t\in[s,T],

(4.13)

and define recursively,

\displaystyle u_{n}(s,x):=K_{2}*w_{n}(s,\cdot)(x),\ \ w_{n}(s,x):={\mathbb{E}}w_{0}(X^{n}_{s,T}(x)).

(4.14)

Clearly, we have
u_{n}\in C([0,T];C^{1}_{b}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}))
and

{\mathbb{E}}\|\nabla X^{n}_{s,t}\|_{\infty}\leqslant 1+\int^{t}_{s}\|\nabla u_{n-1}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\|\nabla X^{n}_{s,r}\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r.

By Gronwall’s inequality we get

{\mathbb{E}}\|\nabla X^{n}_{s,T}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{\int^{T}_{s}\|\nabla u_{n-1}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r}.

Moreover, by (
4.9
) and Lemma
4.4
with
\gamma=1
, we have

\|u_{n}(s)\|_{\infty}+\|\nabla u_{n}(s)\|_{\infty}\lesssim 1+\|w_{n}(s)\|_{\infty}(1+\log(1+\|\nabla w_{n}(s)\|_{\infty})),

and by definition (
4.14
),

\displaystyle\|\nabla w_{n}(s)\|_{\infty}\leqslant\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\|\nabla X^{n}_{s,T}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\int^{T}_{s}\|\nabla u_{n-1}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r}.

(4.15)

Hence,

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{n}(s)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C}1+\|w_{0}\|_{\infty}(1+\log(1+\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\int^{T}_{s}\|\nabla u_{n-1}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r}))

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C}1+\|w_{0}\|_{\infty}\left(1+\log(1+\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{\infty})+\int^{T}_{s}\|\nabla u_{n-1}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right).

By Gronwall’s inequality again, we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{n}\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\|\nabla u_{n}(s)\|_{\infty}\leqslant C.

(4.16)

On the other hand, by (
4.13
) we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\|X^{n}_{s,t}-X^{m}_{s,t}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon{\mathbb{E}}\int^{t}_{s}\|u_{n-1}(r,X^{n}_{s,r-})-u_{n-1}(r,X^{m}_{s,r-})\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{r}

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon{\mathbb{E}}\int^{t}_{s}\|u_{n-1}(r)-u_{m-1}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{r}

\displaystyle\leqslant\int^{t}_{s}\|\nabla u_{n-1}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\|X^{n}_{s,r}-X^{m}_{s,r}\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r+\int^{t}_{s}\|u_{n-1}(r)-u_{m-1}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r,

which together with (
4.16
) implies by Gronwall’s inequality that

\sup_{t\in[s,T]}{\mathbb{E}}\|X^{n}_{s,t}-X^{m}_{s,t}\|_{\infty}\leqslant C\int^{T}_{s}\|u_{n-1}(r)-u_{m-1}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r.

Thus, by (
4.9
) we get

\displaystyle\|w_{n}(s)-w_{m}(s)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\|X^{n}_{s,T}-X^{m}_{s,T}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{\infty}\int^{T}_{s}\|u_{n-1}(r)-u_{m-1}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{\infty}\int^{T}_{s}\|w_{n-1}(r)-w_{m-1}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r.

By Gronwall’s inequality again, we have

\lim_{n,m\to\infty}\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\|w_{n}(s)-w_{m}(s)\|_{\infty}=0,

and also,

\lim_{n,m\to\infty}\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\sup_{t\in[s,T]}{\mathbb{E}}\|X^{n}_{s,t}-X^{m}_{s,t}\|_{\infty}=0.

By taking limits for (
4.13
) and (
4.14
), we obtain the desired result.
Moreover, estimate (
4.12
) follows by (
4.15
) and (
4.16
).
∎

Now we can show the following main result of this section.

Theorem 4.7
.

Suppose that
\varphi\in C^{5}({\mathbb{T}}^{2};{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
is divergence free and satisfies
\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{2}}\varphi(x){\mathord{{\rm d}}}x=0
.
Let
u\in C([0,T];C^{5}({\mathbb{T}}^{2};{\mathbb{R}}^{2}))
be the unique solution of NSE (
4.6
). Then there is a constant
C>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,

\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\|u_{\varepsilon}(s)-u(s)\|_{\infty}\leqslant C\varepsilon.

Proof.

For
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
, let
\widetilde{X}^{\varepsilon}_{s,t}(x)
solve the following SDE on torus
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}^{\varepsilon}_{s,t}(x)=x+\varepsilon\int^{t}_{s}u(r,\widetilde{X}^{\varepsilon}_{s,r-}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\varepsilon}_{r}+\sqrt{\varepsilon\nu}(H^{\varepsilon}_{t}-H^{\varepsilon}_{s}),

(4.17)

where
H^{\varepsilon}_{t}
is a compound Poisson process defined in (
1.13
). Since

u(r,\cdot)
is a function on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
and
u(r,x+z)=u(r,x)
for any
z\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
, one sees that

\widetilde{X}^{\varepsilon}_{s,t}(x+z)=\widetilde{X}^{\varepsilon}_{s,t}(x)+z,\ \ z\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}.

Let
w={\rm curl}(u)
and
w_{0}={\rm curl}(\varphi)
,

\widetilde{w}_{\varepsilon}(s,x):={\mathbb{E}}w_{0}(\widetilde{X}^{\varepsilon}_{s,T}(x)).

By (
4.7
) and Itô’s formula, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{w}_{\varepsilon}(s,x)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}w(T,\widetilde{X}^{\varepsilon}_{s,T}(x))=w(s,x)+{\mathbb{E}}\int^{T}_{s}(\partial_{s}w+\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{r}w)(r,\widetilde{X}^{\varepsilon}_{s,r}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r

\displaystyle=w(s,x)+{\mathbb{E}}\int^{T}_{s}(\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{r}w-\nu\Delta w-u\cdot\nabla w)(r,\widetilde{X}^{\varepsilon}_{s,r}(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r,

where
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}
is the generator of SDE (
4.17
) and given by

\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f(x):=\sum_{i=1,2}\frac{f(x+\sqrt{\varepsilon\nu}e_{i}+\varepsilon u(s,x))+f(x-\sqrt{\varepsilon\nu}e_{i}+\varepsilon u(s,x))-2f(x)}{2\varepsilon}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{w}_{\varepsilon}(s)-w(s)\|_{\infty}\leqslant\int^{T}_{s}\|(\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{r}w-\nu\Delta w-u\cdot\nabla w)(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r.

(4.18)

Noting that for
i=1,2
,

\displaystyle\frac{f(x+\sqrt{\varepsilon\nu}e_{i}+\varepsilon u(s,x))+f(x-\sqrt{\varepsilon\nu}e_{i}+\varepsilon u(s,x))-2f(x+\varepsilon u(s,x))}{2\varepsilon}-\nu\partial^{2}_{i}f(x)

\displaystyle\qquad=\nu\int^{1}_{0}\frac{\theta}{2}\int^{1}_{-1}\Big(\partial^{2}_{i}f(x+\theta\theta^{\prime}\sqrt{\varepsilon\nu}e_{i}+\varepsilon u(s,x))-\partial^{2}_{i}f(x)\Big){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta^{\prime}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta

\displaystyle\qquad=\nu\int^{1}_{0}\frac{\theta}{2}\int^{1}_{-1}\theta\theta^{\prime}\sqrt{\varepsilon\nu}\int^{1}_{0}\partial^{3}_{i}f(x+\theta\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime\prime}\sqrt{\varepsilon\nu}e_{i}+\theta^{\prime\prime}\varepsilon u(s,x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta^{\prime\prime}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta^{\prime}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta

\displaystyle\qquad=\sqrt{\varepsilon\nu^{3}}\int^{1}_{0}\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\int^{1}_{-1}\theta^{\prime}\int^{1}_{0}\Big(\partial^{3}_{i}f(x+\theta\theta^{\prime}\theta^{\prime\prime}\sqrt{\varepsilon\nu}e_{i}+\theta^{\prime\prime}\varepsilon u(s,x))-\partial^{3}_{i}f(x)\Big){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta^{\prime\prime}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta^{\prime}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta

and

\displaystyle\frac{f(x+\varepsilon u(s,x))-f(x)}{\varepsilon}-u(s,x)\cdot\nabla f(x)=u(s,x)\cdot\int^{1}_{0}(\nabla f(x+\theta\varepsilon u(s,x))-\nabla f(x)){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta,

we have

\|\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}^{(\varepsilon)}_{s}f-\nu\Delta f-u\cdot\nabla f\|_{\infty}\lesssim(\varepsilon\nu^{2}+\sqrt{\varepsilon^{3}\nu^{3}}\|u\|_{\infty})\|\nabla^{4}f\|_{\infty}+\varepsilon\|u\|^{2}_{\infty}\|\nabla^{2}f\|_{\infty}.

Substituting this into (
4.18
), we obtain that for all
\varepsilon,\nu\in(0,1]
,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{w}_{\varepsilon}(s)-w(s)\|_{\infty}\lesssim\varepsilon\int^{T}_{s}(1+\|u(r)\|^{2}_{\infty})\|w(r)\|_{C^{4}_{b}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r.

(4.19)

On the other hand, by (
4.17
) and (
4.11
), we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\|\widetilde{X}^{\varepsilon}_{s,t}-X^{\varepsilon}_{s,t}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon{\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{t}_{s}\big\|u(r,\widetilde{X}^{\varepsilon}_{s,r-})-u_{\varepsilon}(r,X^{\varepsilon}_{s,r-})\big\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}_{r}^{\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\int^{t}_{s}\big\|u(r,\widetilde{X}^{\varepsilon}_{s,r})-u_{\varepsilon}(r,X^{\varepsilon}_{s,r})\big\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r

\displaystyle\leqslant\int^{t}_{s}{\mathbb{E}}\big\|u_{\varepsilon}(r,\widetilde{X}^{\varepsilon}_{s,r})-u_{\varepsilon}(r,X^{\varepsilon}_{s,r})\big\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r+\int^{t}_{s}\|u(r)-u_{\varepsilon}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r

\displaystyle\leqslant\int^{t}_{s}\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\|\widetilde{X}^{\varepsilon}_{s,r}-X^{\varepsilon}_{s,r}\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r+\int^{t}_{s}\|u(r)-u_{\varepsilon}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r,

which implies by Gronwall’s inequality that

{\mathbb{E}}\|\widetilde{X}^{\varepsilon}_{s,T}(\cdot)-X^{\varepsilon}_{s,t}(\cdot)\|_{\infty}\lesssim\int^{T}_{s}\|u(r)-u_{\varepsilon}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r,

and

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{w}_{\varepsilon}(s)-w_{\varepsilon}(s)\|_{\infty}\leqslant\|\nabla w_{0}\|_{\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\|\widetilde{X}^{\varepsilon}_{s,T}(\cdot)-X^{\varepsilon}_{s,T}(\cdot)\|_{\infty}\lesssim\int^{T}_{s}\|u(r)-u_{\varepsilon}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r.

Combining this with (
4.19
) and (
4.9
) yields that

\|w_{\varepsilon}(s)-w(s)\|_{\infty}\lesssim\varepsilon+\int^{T}_{s}\|u(r)-u_{\varepsilon}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\lesssim\varepsilon+\int^{T}_{s}\|w(r)-w_{\varepsilon}(r)\|_{\infty}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r.

By Gronwall’s inequality and (
4.9
), we obtain the desired estimate.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

In addition to the 2D-Navier-Stokes equations on the torus, we can also consider the construction of a compound Poisson approximation for 3D-Navier-Stokes equations on the torus.
This will be the focus of our future work. We anticipate that similar convergence results for short time will be obtained in this case as well, following the methodology described in
[
43
]
.

5.
Propagation of chaos for the particle approximation of DDSDEs

In this section, we investigate the propagation of chaos in the context of the interaction particle approximation for McKean-Vlasov SDEs driven by either Brownian motions or
\alpha
-stable processes. The notion of propagation of chaos refers to the convergence of the particle system to the solution of the McKean-Vlasov SDE as the number of particles tends to infinity. This provides a direct full discretization scheme for nonlinear SDEs, allowing for efficient numerical simulations.

Fix an
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a symmetric probability measure
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.
Let
({\mathcal{N}}^{N,i})_{i=1,\cdots,N}
be a sequence of i.i.d. Poisson process with intensity
N

and
(\xi^{N,i}_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},i=1,\cdots,N}
i.i.d
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
-valued random variables with common distribution
\nu
. Define
for
i=1,\cdots,N
,

H^{N,i}_{t}:=\Big(\xi^{N,i}_{1}+\cdots+\xi^{N,i}_{{\mathcal{N}}^{N,i}_{t}}\Big){\mathbbm{1}}_{{\mathcal{N}}^{N,i}_{t}\geqslant 1}.

Then
(H^{N,i})_{i=1,\cdots,N}
is a sequence of i.i.d. compound Poisson processes with intensity
N{\mathord{{\rm d}}}t\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)
.
Let
{\mathcal{H}}^{N,i}
be the associated Poisson random measure, that is,

{\mathcal{H}}^{N,i}([0,t],E):=\sum_{s\leqslant t}{\mathbbm{1}}_{E}(\Delta H^{N,i}_{s})=\sum_{n\leqslant{\mathcal{N}}^{N,i}_{t}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{E}(\xi^{N,i}_{n}),\ \ E\in{\mathscr{B}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),

and
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{N,i}
the compensated Poisson random measure, that is,

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{N,i}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}t,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z):={\mathcal{H}}^{N,i}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}t,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)-N{\mathord{{\rm d}}}t\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z).

For a point
{\mathbf{x}}=(x^{1},\cdots,x^{N})\in({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{N}
, the empirical measure of
{\mathbf{x}}
is defined by

\mu_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x^{i}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),

where
\delta_{x^{i}}
is the usual Dirac measure concentrated at point
x^{i}
.
Let

\sigma_{N}(t,x,y,z):{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\ b_{N}(t,x,y):{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

be two Borel measurable functions. Suppose that

\sigma_{N}(t,x,y,-z)=-\sigma_{N}(t,x,y,z).

For a probability measure
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, we write

\sigma_{N}[t,x,\mu,z]:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sigma_{N}(t,x,y,z)\mu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}y),\ \ b_{N}[t,x,\mu]:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}b_{N}(t,x,y)\mu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}y).

Let
{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{t}=(X^{N,i}_{t})_{i=1,\cdots,N}

solve the following interaction particle system driven by
{\mathcal{H}}^{N,i}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}X^{N,i}_{t}&=X^{N,i}_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left(\sigma_{N}{\Big[}s,X^{N,i}_{s-},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}},z{\Big]}+b_{N}{\Big[}s,X^{N,i}_{s-},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}}{\Big]}\right){\mathcal{H}}^{N,i}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\\
&=X^{N,i}_{t-}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(\sigma_{N}{\big(}t,X^{N,i}_{t-},X^{N,j}_{t-},\Delta H^{N,i}_{t}{\big)}+b_{N}{\big(}t,X^{N,i}_{t-},X^{N,j}_{t-}{\big)}\Delta{\mathcal{N}}^{N,i}_{t}\right),\end{split}

(5.1)

where
{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{0}
is a symmetric
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
-measurable random variables.
For a function
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, by Itô’s formula (see (
3.12
)), we have

\displaystyle f(X^{N,i}_{t})=f(X^{N,i}_{0})+\int^{t}_{0}{\mathscr{L}}^{N}_{\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s}}}f(s,X^{N,i}_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+\int^{t}_{0}\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\Theta^{N}_{\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}}}f(s,X^{N,i}_{s-},z)\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{N,i}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z),

(5.2)

where for a probability measure
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,

\displaystyle{\mathscr{L}}^{N}_{\mu}f(t,x):={\mathscr{L}}^{N}_{t,\mu}f(x):=N\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\Big(f{\big(}x+\sigma_{N}[t,x,\mu,z]+b_{N}[t,x,\mu]{\big)}-f(x)\Big)\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z),

(5.3)

and

\displaystyle\Theta^{N}_{\mu}f(t,x,z):=\Theta^{N}_{t,\mu}f(x,z):=f{\big(}x+\sigma_{N}[t,x,\mu,z]+b_{N}[t,x,\mu]{\big)}-f(x).

(5.4)

As in Section 2, we write

{\mathscr{L}}^{N}_{\mu}f(t,x)={\mathcal{A}}^{N}_{\mu}f(t,x)+{\mathcal{B}}^{N}_{\mu}f(t,x),

where

{\mathcal{A}}^{N}_{\mu}f(t,x):=N\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\Big({\mathcal{D}}^{N}_{\mu}f{\big(}t,x+\sigma_{N}[t,x,\mu,z]{\big)}-{\mathcal{D}}^{N}_{\mu}f(t,x)\Big)\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z),

and

{\mathcal{B}}^{N}_{\mu}f(t,x):=N({\mathcal{D}}^{N}_{\mu}f(t,x)-f(x)),\ \ {\mathcal{D}}^{N}_{\mu}f(t,x):=f{\big(}x+b_{N}[t,x,\mu]{\big)}.

Note that by the symmetry of
\nu
and
\sigma_{N}(t,x,y,-z)=-\sigma_{N}(t,x,y,z).
,

{\mathcal{A}}^{N}_{\mu}f(t,x):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{{\mathcal{D}}^{N}_{\mu}f{\big(}t,x+\sigma_{N}[t,x,\mu,z]{\big)}+{\mathcal{D}}^{N}_{\mu}f{\big(}t,x-\sigma_{N}[t,x,\mu,z]{\big)}-2{\mathcal{D}}^{N}_{\mu}f(t,x)}{2N^{-1}}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z).

We shall give precise choices of
\sigma_{N}
and
b_{N}
below in different cases.

5.1.
Fractional diffusion with bounded interaction kernel

In this section we fix
\alpha\in(0,2)
and let

\sigma(t,x,y,z):{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\ b(t,x,y):{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

be two Borel measurable functions. We make the following assumptions:

\rm({\bf H}^{\sigma,b}_{\nu,\alpha})

In addition to (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
) with
\alpha\in(0,2)
, we suppose that
\sigma
and
b
are continuous in
(x,y)
, and

\sigma(t,x,y,-z)=-\sigma(t,x,y,z),\ \ |\sigma(t,x,y,z)|\leqslant(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}|x|)|z|,

and for the same
\beta_{0}
as in (
3.5
),

|\sigma(t,x,y,z)-\sigma(t,x,y,z^{\prime})|\leqslant(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}|x|)(|z-z^{\prime}|\wedge 1)^{\beta_{0}},

where
\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1}>0
. Moreover, for some
m\geqslant 1
and
\kappa_{2}>0
,

\displaystyle|b(t,x,y)|\leqslant{\big(}\kappa_{2}(1+|x|){\big)}^{m},

(5.5)

and for some
\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4}\geqslant 0
and
\kappa_{5}<0
,

\displaystyle\langle x,b(t,x,y)\rangle\leqslant\kappa_{3}+\kappa_{4}|x|^{2}+\kappa_{5}|x|^{m+1}.

(5.6)

In the above assumptions, we have assumed boundedness of the coefficients with respect to the variable
y
, which imposes a restriction on the interaction kernel. However, in the next subsection, we relax this assumption and consider the case of unbounded kernels.
Now, we introduce the approximation coefficients
\sigma_{N}
and
b_{N}
as defined in (
3.19
).

\displaystyle\sigma_{N}(t,x,y,z):=\sigma{\big(}t,x,y,N^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}z{\big)},\ \ b_{N}(t,x,y):=\frac{b(t,x,y)}{N+\sqrt{N}|b(t,x,y)|^{1-\frac{1}{m}}},

(5.7)

and also define for
t\geqslant 0
and
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,

\displaystyle{\mathscr{L}}^{\infty}_{\mu}f(t,x):={\mathscr{L}}^{\infty}_{t,\mu}f(x):={\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\mu}f(t,x)+b[t,x,\mu]\cdot\nabla f(x),

(5.8)

where

{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\mu}f(t,x):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{f(x+\sigma[t,x,\mu,z])+f(x-\sigma[t,x,\mu,z])-2f(x)}{2}\nu_{0}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z),

and
\nu_{0}
is the Lévy measure from (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
).
We consider the following McKean-Vlasov SDE:

\displaystyle{\mathord{{\rm d}}}X_{t}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sigma\big[t,X_{t-},\mu_{X_{t-}},z\big]\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}t,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)+b[t,X_{t},\mu_{X_{t}}]{\mathord{{\rm d}}}t,

(5.9)

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
is defined as (
3.23
) and
\mu_{X_{t}}
is the law of
X_{t}
. By Itô’s formula,
the nonlinear time-inhomogeneous infinitesimal generator of
X_{t}
is given by
{\mathscr{L}}^{\infty}_{t,\mu_{X_{t}}}
.

The following lemma is the same as Lemma
3.9
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Under
\rm({\bf H}^{\sigma,b}_{\nu,\alpha})
, where
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
for any
R>0
, there is a constant
C_{R}>0
such that
for any
f\in C^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\sup_{t\geqslant 0}\sup_{|x|\leqslant R}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\big|{\mathscr{L}}^{N}_{t,\mu}f(x)-{\mathscr{L}}^{\infty}_{t,\mu}f(x)\big|\leqslant C_{R}N^{-\frac{2-\alpha}{2}\wedge\beta_{1}}\|f\|_{C^{2}_{b}},

where
\beta_{1}
is from (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
).
Moreover, if
b
is bounded measurable and
\kappa_{1}=0
, then
C_{R}
can be independent of
R>0
.

Proof.

Below we drop the time variable for simplicity.
Recall that

{\mathcal{B}}^{N}_{\mu}f(x)=N(f(x+b_{N}[x,\mu])-f(x)).

By Taylor’s expansion and the definition (
5.7
), we have

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{B}}^{N}_{\mu}f(x)-b[x,\mu]\cdot\nabla f(x)|

\displaystyle\leqslant|{\mathcal{B}}^{N}_{\mu}f(x)-Nb_{N}[x,\mu]\cdot\nabla f(x)|+|(Nb_{N}[x,\mu]-b[x,\mu])\cdot\nabla f(x)|

\displaystyle\leqslant N|b_{N}[x,\mu]|\int^{1}_{0}(|\nabla f(x+\theta b_{N}[x,\mu])-\nabla f(x)|){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta

\displaystyle\quad+|Nb_{N}[x,\mu]-b[x,\mu]|\cdot\|\nabla f\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant N|b_{N}[x,\mu]|^{2}\|\nabla^{2}f\|_{\infty}+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{\sqrt{N}|b(x,y)|^{2-\frac{1}{m}}}{N+\sqrt{N}|b(x,y)|^{1-\frac{1}{m}}}\mu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}y)\|\nabla f\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left(\frac{|b(x,y)|^{2}}{N}+\frac{|b(x,y)|^{2-\frac{1}{m}}}{\sqrt{N}}\right)\mu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}y)\|\nabla f\|_{C^{1}_{b}}.

Under (
5.5
), we clearly have

\sup_{|x|\leqslant R}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}|{\mathcal{B}}^{N}_{\mu}f(x)-b[x,\mu]\cdot\nabla f(x)|\leqslant C_{R}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla f\|_{C^{1}_{b}}.

Moreover, as in (
3.29
) we also have

\sup_{|x|\leqslant R}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\big|{\mathcal{A}}^{N}_{\mu}f(x)-{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\mu}f(x)\big|\leqslant C_{R}N^{-(1-\frac{\alpha}{2})\wedge\beta_{1}}\|f\|_{C^{2}_{b}}.

Combining the above two estimates, we obtain the desired estimate.
∎

The following lemma is similar to Lemma
3.12
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Under
\rm({\bf H}^{\sigma,b}_{\nu,\alpha})
, where
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
for any
\beta\in(0,\alpha)
,
there are constants
N_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
C_{0}=C_{0}(\beta)>0
,
C_{1}=C_{1}(\beta,\nu)>0

and
C_{2}>0
such that for all
N\geqslant N_{0}
,
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})

and
t\geqslant 0
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

{\mathscr{L}}^{N}_{t,\mu}U_{\beta}(x)\leqslant(C_{0}\kappa_{6}+C_{1}\kappa^{\alpha}_{1})U_{\beta}(x)+C_{2},

where
U_{\beta}(x)=(1+|x|^{2})^{\beta/2}
and
\kappa_{6}
is given in (
3.30
).

Proof.

For simplicity we drop the time variable. For
{\mathcal{B}}^{N}_{\mu}U_{\beta}(x)
, by Taylor’s expansion we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}^{N}_{\mu}U_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=N\int^{1}_{0}\langle b_{N}[x,\mu],\nabla U_{\beta}(x+\theta b_{N}[x,\mu])\rangle{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta

\displaystyle=\beta N\int^{1}_{0}\langle b_{N}[x,\mu],x+\theta b_{N}[x,\mu]\rangle U_{\beta-2}(x+\theta b_{N}[x,\mu]){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta.

(5.10)

By (
5.5
) and (
5.6
), for any
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, there are
N_{0}
large enough so that for all
N\geqslant N_{0}
,

\displaystyle|b_{N}(x,y)|\leqslant N^{-1/2}|b(x,y)|^{\frac{1}{m}}\leqslant N^{-1/2}\kappa_{2}(1+|x|)\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}(1+|x|),

(5.11)

and as in Lemma
3.11
, for the
\kappa_{6}
given in (
3.30
),

N\langle x,b_{N}(x,y)\rangle+N|b_{N}(x,y)|^{2}\leqslant\kappa_{6}|x|^{2}+C_{1}.

Thus, for all
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
\theta\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle N\langle b_{N}[x,\mu],x+\theta b_{N}[x,\mu]\rangle\leqslant N\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\big(}\langle b_{N}(x,y),x\rangle+|b_{N}(x,y)|^{2}{\big)}\mu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}y)\leqslant\kappa_{6}|x|^{2}+C_{1}

and

(1+|x|^{2})/2\leqslant 1+|x+\theta b_{N}[x,\mu]|^{2}\leqslant 2(1+|x|^{2}).

Hence, as in (
3.36
), we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}^{N}_{\mu}U_{\beta}(x)\leqslant C_{0}\kappa_{6}U_{\beta}(x)+C.

For
{\mathcal{A}}^{N}_{\mu}U_{\beta}(x)
, as in (
3.37
) we also have

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{A}}^{N}_{\mu}U_{\beta}(x)|\leqslant C_{0}\kappa_{1}^{\alpha}U_{\beta}(x)+C.

Combining the above two estimates, we obtain the desired estimate.
∎

By the above Lyapunov estimate and Itô’s formula, the following corollary is the same as Corollary
3.14
. We omit the details.

Corollary 5.3
.

Under
\rm({\bf H}^{\sigma,b}_{\nu,\alpha})
, for any
\beta\in(0,\alpha)
and
T>0
, there is a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{i=1,\cdots,N}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}U_{\beta}(X^{N,i}_{t})\right)\leqslant C(1+{\mathbb{E}}U_{\beta}(X_{0})),

(5.12)

where
U_{\beta}(x)=(1+|x|^{2})^{\beta/2}
.
Moreover, there is a constant
C_{2}>0
such that for all
t>0
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}U_{\beta}(X^{N,i}_{t})\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{\kappa_{7}t}{\mathbb{E}}U_{\beta}(X_{0})+C_{2}(\mathrm{e}^{\kappa_{7}t}-1)/\kappa_{7},

(5.13)

where
\kappa_{7}:=C_{0}\kappa_{6}+C_{1}\kappa^{\alpha}_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}
(see Lemma
5.2
).

The following lemma is similar to Lemma
3.15
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Under
\rm({\bf H}^{\sigma,b}_{\nu,\alpha})
, for any
T,\gamma>0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\sup_{N}\sup_{\tau\leqslant\eta\leqslant\tau+\delta\leqslant T}{\mathbb{P}}\Big(|X^{N,1}_{\eta}-X^{N,1}_{\tau}|\geqslant\gamma\Big)=0.

(5.14)

Proof.

Let
\tau,\eta\in{\mathscr{T}}_{T}
with
\tau\leqslant\eta\leqslant\tau+\delta
. For fixed
R>0
, define

\zeta_{R}:=\inf\left\{t>0:|X^{N,1}_{t}|>R\right\},\ \ \tau_{R}:=\zeta_{R}\wedge\tau,\ \eta_{R}:=\zeta_{R}\wedge\eta.

By (
5.1
), we can write

\displaystyle X^{N,1}_{\eta_{R}}-X^{N,1}_{\tau_{R}}

\displaystyle=\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}b_{N}{\Big[}s,X^{N,1}_{s-},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}}{\Big]}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{N,1}_{s}+\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{|z|<N^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\sigma_{N}{\Big[}s,X^{N,1}_{s-},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}},z{\Big]}{\mathcal{H}}^{N,1}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle\quad+\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{|z|>N^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\sigma_{N}{\Big[}s,X^{N,1}_{s-},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}},z{\Big]}{\mathcal{H}}^{N,1}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=:I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}.

For
I_{1}
, by (
5.5
) and
\eta_{R}-\tau_{R}\leqslant\delta
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|I_{1}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{N}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\Big|b{\Big[}s,X^{N,1}_{s-},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}}{\Big]}\Big|{\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{N,1}_{s}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\Big|b{\Big[}s,X^{N,1}_{s},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s}}{\Big]}\Big|{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)\leqslant C_{R}\delta.

For
I_{2}
, by (
3.10
) and the isometry of stochastic integrals, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|I_{2}|^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left|\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{|z|<N^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\sigma_{N}{\Big[}s,X^{N,1}_{s-},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}},z{\Big]}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{N,1}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{|z|<N^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\Big|\sigma_{N}{\Big[}s,X^{N,1}_{s},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}},z{\Big]}\Big|^{2}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}(Ns){\big)}\right).

Let
\nu_{N}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=N\nu(N^{1/\alpha}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z).
By the change of variables, we further have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|I_{2}|^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{|z|<1}\Big|\sigma{\Big[}s,X^{N,1}_{s},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}},z{\Big]}\Big|^{2}\nu_{N}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}R)^{2}\left(\int_{|z|<1}|z|^{2}\nu_{N}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right)\delta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{VV9}}}{{\leqslant}}C_{R}\delta.

For
I_{3}
, let
\beta\in(0,\alpha\wedge 1)
. By
|\sum_{i}a_{i}|^{\beta}\leqslant\sum_{i}|a_{i}|^{\beta}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|I_{3}|^{\beta}

\displaystyle\leqslant{\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{|z|\geqslant N^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\Big|\sigma_{N}{\Big[}s,X^{N,1}_{s},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}},z{\Big]}\Big|^{\beta}{\mathcal{H}}^{N,1}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{|z|\geqslant N^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}}\Big|\sigma_{N}{\Big[}s,X^{N,1}_{s},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}},z{\Big]}\Big|^{\beta}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}(Ns)\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{|z|\geqslant 1}\Big|\sigma{\Big[}s,X^{N,1}_{s},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}},z{\Big]}\Big|\nu_{N}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}R)^{\beta}\left(\int_{|z|\geqslant 1}|z|^{\beta}\nu_{N}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right)\delta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{VV9}}}{{\leqslant}}C_{R}\delta.

Hence, by Chebyshev’s inequality and (
5.12
),

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(|X^{N,1}_{\eta}-X^{N,1}_{\tau}|\geqslant\gamma)

\displaystyle\leqslant{\mathbb{P}}(|X^{N,1}_{\eta_{R}}-X^{N,1}_{\tau_{R}}|\geqslant\gamma;\zeta_{R}>T)+{\mathbb{P}}(\zeta_{R}\leqslant T)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{3}{\mathbb{P}}(|I_{i}|\geqslant\tfrac{\gamma}{3})+{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|X^{N,1}_{t}|\geqslant R\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\tfrac{3}{\gamma}{\mathbb{E}}|I_{1}|+(\tfrac{3}{\gamma})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}|I_{2}|^{2}+(\tfrac{3}{\gamma})^{\beta}{\mathbb{E}}|I_{3}|^{\beta}+\tfrac{C}{R^{\beta}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{R,\gamma}\delta+C/R^{\beta},

which converges to zero by firstly letting
\delta\to 0
and then
R\to\infty
.
∎

Now we can show the following main result of this subsection about the propagation of chaos.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
\mu_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Suppose that for any
k\leqslant N
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\circ{\big(}X^{N,1}_{0},\cdots,X^{N,k}_{0}{\big)}^{-1}\to\mu_{0}^{\otimes k},\ \ N\to\infty,

(5.15)

and DDSDE (
5.9
) admits a unique martingale solution
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\mu_{0}}_{0}({\mathscr{L}}^{\infty})

with initial distribution
\mu_{0}
in the sense of Definition
6.2
in appendix.
Then under
\rm({\bf H}^{\sigma,b}_{\nu,\alpha})
, for any
k\leqslant N
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\circ{\big(}X^{N,1}_{\cdot},\cdots,X^{N,k}_{\cdot}{\big)}^{-1}\to{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{\otimes k},\ \ N\to\infty.

(5.16)

Proof.

We use the classical martingale method (see
[
18
]
).
Consider the following random measure with values in
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}})
,

\omega\to\Pi_{N}(\omega,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}w):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X^{N,i}_{\cdot}(\omega)}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}w)\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}}).

By Lemma
5.4
, Aldous’ criterion (see
[
23
]
) and
[
40
, (ii) of Proposition 2.2]
, the law of
\Pi_{N}
in
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}}))
is tight.
Without loss of generality, we assume that the law of
\Pi_{N}
weakly converges to some
\Pi_{\infty}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}}))
.
Our aim below is to show that
\Pi_{\infty}
is a Dirac measure, i.e.,

\Pi_{\infty}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}\eta)=\delta_{{\mathbb{P}}_{0}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}\eta),\ \ \Pi_{\infty}-a.s.,

where
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{\mu_{0}}({\mathscr{L}}^{\infty})
is the unique martingale solution of DDSDE (
5.9
).
If we can show the above assertion, then by
[
40
, (i) of Proposition 2.2]
, we conclude (
5.16
).

Let
f\in C^{2}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. For given
\eta\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}})
, we define a functional
M^{f}_{\eta}(t,\cdot)
on
{\mathbb{D}}
by

M^{f}_{\eta}(t,w):=f(w_{t})-f(w_{0})-\int^{t}_{0}{\mathscr{L}}^{\infty}_{s,\eta}f(w_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,\ t\geqslant 0,\ w\in{\mathbb{D}},

where
{\mathscr{L}}^{\infty}_{s,\eta}
is defined by (
5.8
) with
\mu=\eta_{s}
.
Fix
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
s\leqslant t
.
For given
g\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{nd})
and
0\leqslant s_{1}<\cdots<s_{n}\leqslant s
, we also introduce a functional
\Xi^{g}_{f}
over
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}})
by

\Xi^{g}_{f}(\eta):=\int_{{\mathbb{D}}}{\big(}M^{f}_{\eta}(t,w)-M^{f}_{\eta}(s,w){\big)}g(w_{s_{1}},\cdots,w_{s_{n}})\eta({\mathord{{\rm d}}}w).

By definition we have

\Xi^{g}_{f}(\eta)=\int_{{\mathbb{D}}}\left(f(w_{t})-f(w_{s})-\int^{t}_{s}{\mathscr{L}}^{\infty}_{r,\eta}f(w_{r}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right)g(w_{s_{1}},\cdots,w_{s_{n}})\eta({\mathord{{\rm d}}}w)

and

\displaystyle\Xi^{g}_{f}(\Pi_{N})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\left(f(X^{N,i}_{t})-f(X^{N,i}_{s})-\int^{t}_{s}{\mathscr{L}}^{\infty}_{r,\Pi_{N}}f(X^{N,i}_{r}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right)g{\big(}X^{N,i}_{s_{1}},\cdots,X^{N,i}_{s_{n}}{\big)}\right].

(5.17)

By definition (
5.8
) and
\rm({\bf H}^{\sigma,b}_{\nu,\alpha})
, it is easy to see that

\eta\mapsto\Xi^{g}_{f}(\eta)
is bounded continuous on
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}})
.

Hence, by the weak convergence of
\Pi_{N}
to
\Pi_{\infty}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}|\Xi^{g}_{f}(\Pi_{N})|=\int_{{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}})}|\Xi^{g}_{f}(\eta)|\Pi_{\infty}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}\eta).

(5.18)

On the other hand, let

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Xi}^{g}_{f}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\left(f(X^{N,i}_{t})-f(X^{N,i}_{s})-\int^{t}_{s}{\mathscr{L}}^{N}_{r,\Pi_{N}}f(X^{N,i}_{r}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right)g{\big(}X^{N,i}_{s_{1}},\cdots,X^{N,i}_{s_{n}}{\big)}\right],

(5.19)

where
{\mathscr{L}}^{N}_{\Pi_{N}}
is defined by (
5.3
).
By Itô’s formula (
5.2
), we have

\widetilde{\Xi}^{g}_{f}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\left(\int^{t}_{s}\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\Theta^{N}_{r,\Pi_{N}}f(X^{N,i}_{r-},z)\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{N,i}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}r,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right)g{\big(}X^{N,i}_{s_{1}},\cdots,X^{N,i}_{s_{n}}{\big)}\right],

where
\Theta^{N}_{r,\Pi_{N}}f
is defined by (
5.4
).
By the isometry of stochastic integrals,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|\widetilde{\Xi}^{g}_{f}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\|g\|^{2}_{\infty}}{N}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{s}\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\Theta^{N}_{r,\Pi_{N}}f(X^{N,i}_{r},z)|^{2}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right).

Let
\beta\in(0,\frac{\alpha}{2})
. Noting that by (
5.4
), (
5.11
) and
|\sigma(r,x,y,z)|\leqslant(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}|x|)|z|
,

\displaystyle|\Theta^{N}_{r,\eta}f(x,z)|

\displaystyle\leqslant(|b_{N}[r,x,\eta_{r-}]|^{\beta}+|\sigma_{N}[r,x,\eta_{r-},z]|)^{\beta}\|f\|_{C^{\beta}_{b}}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\big(}\tfrac{1+|x|^{\beta}}{N^{\beta/2}}+(1+|x|^{\beta})\tfrac{|z|^{\beta}}{N^{\beta/\alpha}}{\big)}\|f\|_{C^{\beta}_{b}},

by Lemma
3.3
and (
5.12
), we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|\widetilde{\Xi}^{g}_{f}|^{2}\lesssim\frac{\|g\|^{2}_{\infty}\|f\|_{C^{\beta}_{b}}^{2}}{N^{1+\beta}}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{s}{\big(}1+{\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,i}_{r}|^{2\beta}{\big)}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)\lesssim\frac{1}{N^{\beta}},

(5.20)

where the implicit constant does not depend on
N
.

Claim:
The following limit holds:

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}|\Xi^{g}_{f}(\Pi_{N})-\widetilde{\Xi}^{g}_{f}|=0.

(5.21)

Indeed, by definition (
5.17
), (
5.19
) and Lemma
5.1
, for any
R\geqslant 1
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|\Xi^{g}_{f}(\Pi_{N})-\widetilde{\Xi}^{g}_{f}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{t}_{s}|{\mathscr{L}}^{\infty}_{r,\Pi_{N}}f-{\mathscr{L}}^{N}_{r,\Pi_{N}}f|(X^{N,i}_{r}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right)\|g\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sup_{r\leqslant t}\sup_{|x|\leqslant R}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}|{\mathscr{L}}^{\infty}_{r,\mu}f(x)-{\mathscr{L}}^{N}_{r,\mu}f(x)|\cdot\|g\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle+\sup_{r\leqslant t}\Big(\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}{\big(}\|{\mathscr{L}}^{\infty}_{r,\mu}f\|_{\infty}+\|{\mathscr{L}}^{N}_{r,\mu}f\|_{\infty}{\big)}\sup_{i}{\mathbb{P}}(|X^{N,i}_{r}|\geqslant R)\Big)\|g\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{R}N^{-(\frac{2-\alpha}{2})\wedge\beta_{1}}+C\sup_{i}\sup_{r\leqslant t}{\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,i}_{r}|^{\beta}/R^{\beta},

which yields (
5.21
) by (
5.12
).

Combining (
5.18
), (
5.20
) and (
5.21
) we obtain
that for each
f\in C^{2}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
g\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{nd})
,

\int_{{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}})}|\Xi^{g}_{f}(\eta)|\Pi_{\infty}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}\eta)=0\Rightarrow\Xi^{g}_{f}(\eta)=0\mbox{ for $\Pi_{\infty}$-a.s. $\eta\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}})$}.

Since
C^{2}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{nd})
are separable, one can find a common
\Pi_{\infty}
-null set
{\mathcal{Q}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}})

such that for all
\eta\notin{\mathcal{Q}}
and for all
0\leqslant s<t\leqslant T
,
f\in C_{c}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
g\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{nd})
,

0\leqslant s_{1}<\cdots<s_{n}\leqslant s
,

\Xi^{g}_{f}(\eta)=\int_{{\mathbb{D}}}{\big(}M^{f}_{\eta}(t,w)-M^{f}_{\eta}(s,w){\big)}g(w_{s_{1}},\cdots,w_{s_{n}})\eta({\mathord{{\rm d}}}w)=0.

Moreover, by (
5.15
), we also have

\Pi_{\infty}\{\eta\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}}):\eta_{0}=\mu_{0}\}=1.

Thus by the definition of
{\mathcal{M}}^{\mu_{0}}_{0}({\mathscr{L}}^{\infty})
(see Definition
6.2
in appendix), for
\Pi_{\infty}
-almost all
\eta\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}})
,

\eta\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\mu_{0}}_{0}({\mathscr{L}}^{\infty}).

Since
{\mathcal{M}}^{\mu_{0}}_{0}({\mathscr{L}}^{\infty})
only contains one point by uniqueness, all the points
\eta\notin{\mathcal{Q}}
are the same.
Hence,
\Pi_{N}
weakly converges to the one-point measure
\delta_{{\mathbb{P}}_{0}}
. The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

For each
{\mathbf{x}}=(x^{1},\cdots,x^{N})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{Nd}
, let
{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{t}={\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{t}({\mathbf{x}})

be the unique solution of SDE (
5.1
) with starting point
{\mathbf{x}}
.
Suppose that
\kappa_{7}<0
(see (
5.13
)). Then for each
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the semigroup
P^{N}_{t}f({\mathbf{x}}):={\mathbb{E}}f({\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{t}({\mathbf{x}}))

admits an invariant probability measure
\mu_{N}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathbf{x}})
, which is symmetric in the sense

\mu_{N}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}\pi_{N}({\mathbf{x}}))=\mu_{N}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathbf{x}}),\ \ \mbox{ $\pi_{N}({\mathbf{x}})$ is any permutation of ${\mathbf{x}}=(x^{1},\cdots,x^{N})$}.

Indeed, by (
5.13
), for any
\beta\in(0,\alpha)
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{N}\sup_{i=1,\cdots,N}\sup_{T>0}\frac{1}{T}\int^{T}_{0}{\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,i}_{t}|^{\beta}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}t<\infty.

(5.22)

Now we define a probability measure
\mu_{N,T}
over
{\mathbb{R}}^{Nd}
by

\mu_{N,T}(A):=\frac{1}{T}\int^{T}_{0}{\mathbb{P}}({\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{t}\in A){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t,\ \ A\in{\mathscr{B}}({\mathbb{R}}^{Nd}).

By (
5.22
), the family of probability measures
\{\mu_{N,T},T\geqslant 1\}
is tight.
By the classical Krylov-Bogoliubov argument (cf.
[
10
, Section 3.1]
), any accumulation point
\mu_{N}
of
\{\mu_{N,T},T\geqslant 1\}
is an invariant probability measure of
P^{N}_{t}
, that is,
for any nonnegative measurable function
f
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{Nd}
,

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{Nd}}f({\mathbf{x}})\mu_{N}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathbf{x}})=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{Nd}}P^{N}_{t}f({\mathbf{x}})\mu_{N}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathbf{x}}),\ \ t>0.

The symmetry of
\mu_{N}
follows from the symmetry of
{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{t}
. Moreover, by (
5.22
) one sees that

\sup_{N}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|x|^{\beta}\mu^{(1)}_{N}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x)<\infty,

where
\mu^{(1)}_{N}
is the
1
-marginal distribution of
\mu_{N}
.

Note that the existence of invariant probability measures for DDSDE (
5.9
) has been investigated in
[
16
]
under dissipativity assumptions. However, an open question remains regarding the conditions under which any accumulation point of
{\mu^{(1)}_{N},N\in{\mathbb{N}}}
becomes an invariant probability measure of DDSDE (
5.9
). This question is closely connected to the problem of propagation of chaos in uniform time, as discussed in
[
29
]
.
In future research, we plan to address this question and explore the assumptions on the coefficients that lead to convergence of empirical measures and the emergence of invariant probability measures for DDSDE (
5.9
). Such investigations will contribute to a deeper understanding of the dynamics and statistical properties of DDSDEs and their particle approximations.

5.2.
Brownian diffusion with unbounded interaction kernel

In the previous section, we focused on interaction terms that are bounded in the second variable
y
, which excluded unbounded interaction kernels such as
b(x,y)=\bar{b}(x-y)
, where
\bar{b}
exhibits linear growth. In this section, we address the case of unbounded interaction kernels in the context of Brownian diffusion.
Our results provide insights into the behavior of DDSDEs with unbounded interaction kernels and broaden the applicability of compound Poisson approximations in modeling and numerical simulations.

Fix
\alpha>2
. We make the following assumptions about
\sigma
and
b
:

\rm({\bf\widetilde{H}}^{\sigma,b}_{\nu,\alpha})

We suppose that (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
) holds, and
\sigma
and
b
are continuous in
(x,y)
, and for some
\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1}\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\sigma(t,x,y,-z)=-\sigma(t,x,y,z),\ \ |\sigma(t,x,y,z)|\leqslant(\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}(|x|+|y|))|z|.

(5.23)

Suppose that

b(t,x,y)=b_{1}(t,x)+b_{2}(t,x,y),

where for some
m\geqslant 1
and
\kappa_{2}>0
,

\displaystyle|b_{1}(t,x)|\leqslant{\big(}\kappa_{2}(1+|x|){\big)}^{m},

(5.24)

and for some
\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4}\geqslant 0
and
\kappa_{5}<0
,

\displaystyle\langle x,b_{1}(t,x)\rangle\leqslant\kappa_{3}+\kappa_{4}|x|^{2}+\kappa_{5}|x|^{m+1},

(5.25)

and for some
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
,

\displaystyle|b_{2}(t,x,y)|\leqslant c_{1}+c_{2}|x|+c_{3}|y|.

(5.26)

As in (
3.19
), we introduce the approximation coefficients of
\sigma_{N}
and
b_{N}
as:

\sigma_{N}(t,x,y,z):=N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sigma(t,x,y,z),

and

\displaystyle b_{N}(t,x,y):=\frac{b_{1}(t,x)}{N+\sqrt{N}|b_{1}(t,x)|^{1-\frac{1}{m}}}+\frac{b_{2}(t,x,y)}{N}.

(5.27)

For
t\geqslant 0
and
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, we also define

\displaystyle{\mathscr{L}}^{\infty}_{\mu}f(t,x):={\mathscr{L}}^{\infty}_{t,\mu}f(x):={\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\mu}f(t,x)+b[t,x,\mu]\cdot\nabla f(x),

(5.28)

where

{\mathcal{A}}^{\infty}_{\mu}f(s,x):=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\Big(\sigma[t,x,\mu,z]\otimes\sigma[t,x,\mu,z]\Big)\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\cdot\nabla^{2}f(x)\right).

Consider the following McKean-Vlasov SDE:

\displaystyle{\mathord{{\rm d}}}X_{t}=\sigma_{\nu}^{(2)}{\big(}t,X_{t},\mu_{X_{t}}{\big)}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}W_{t}+b[t,X_{t},\mu_{X_{t}}]{\mathord{{\rm d}}}t,

(5.29)

where
W_{t}
is a
d
-dimensional standard Brownian motion, and

\sigma_{\nu}^{(2)}(t,x,\mu):=\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sigma[t,x,\mu,z]\otimes\sigma[t,x,\mu,z]\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

By Itô’s formula, the nonlinear time-inhomogeneous generator of DDSDE (
5.29
) is given by
{\mathscr{L}}^{\infty}_{t,\mu}
.

The following lemma is the same as Lemmas
5.1
and
3.9
. We omit the details.

Lemma 5.7
.

Under
\rm({\bf\widetilde{H}}^{\sigma,b}_{\nu,\alpha})
, where
\alpha>2
,
for any
R>0
, there is a constant
C_{R}>0
such that
for any
f\in C^{\alpha}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, and for all
N
and
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
with
\mu(|\cdot|)\leqslant R
,

\sup_{t\geqslant 0,|x|\leqslant R}\big|{\mathscr{L}}^{N}_{t,\mu}f(x)-{\mathscr{L}}^{\infty}_{t,\mu}f(x)\big|\leqslant C_{R}N^{-\frac{(\alpha-2)\wedge 1}{2}}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha\wedge 3}_{b}}.

Moreover, if
b
is bounded measurable and
\kappa_{1}=0
, then
C_{R}
can be independent of
R>0
.

The following lemma is similar to Lemma
5.2
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Under
\rm({\bf\widetilde{H}}^{\sigma,b}_{\nu,\alpha})
, where
\alpha>2
,
for any
\beta\in[2,\alpha]
, there are constants
C_{0},C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that for all
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\displaystyle{\mathscr{L}}^{N}_{s,\mu}(|\cdot|^{\beta})(x)\leqslant C_{0}|x|^{\beta}+C_{1}\mu(|\cdot|)^{\beta}+C_{2}.

(5.30)

Moreover, if
m>1
, then for any
\kappa_{6}<0
, there are constants
N_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
C_{3}=C_{3}(\beta,\nu)>0
and
C_{4}=C_{4}(N_{0},\kappa_{i},c_{i})>0

such that for all
N\geqslant N_{0}
,
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})

and
s\geqslant 0
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

\displaystyle{\mathscr{L}}^{N}_{s,\mu}(|\cdot|^{\beta})(x)\leqslant\kappa_{6}|x|^{\beta}+(\beta c_{3}+C_{3}\kappa^{\beta}_{1})\mu(|\cdot|)^{\beta}+C_{4}.

(5.31)

Proof.

We only prove (
5.31
). For simplicity we drop the time variable.
By (
5.24
)-(
5.26
), we have

\displaystyle N\langle x,b_{N}[x,\mu]\rangle+N|b_{N}[x,\mu]|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\kappa_{3}+\kappa_{4}|x|^{2}+\kappa_{5}|x|^{m+1}}{1+\sqrt{N^{-1}}|b_{1}(x)|^{1-\frac{1}{m}}}+|x|\cdot(c_{1}+c_{2}|x|+c_{3}\mu(|\cdot|))

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2\kappa^{2}_{2}(1+|x|)^{2}}{N}+\frac{2(c_{1}+c_{2}|x|+c_{3}\mu(|\cdot|))^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\kappa_{5}|x|^{m+1}}{1+\sqrt{N^{-1}}|b_{1}(x)|^{1-\frac{1}{m}}}+C_{0}+C_{1}|x|^{2}+\big(\tfrac{c_{3}}{4}+\tfrac{4c_{3}}{N}\big)\mu(|\cdot|)^{2}.

Since
m>1
and
\kappa_{5}<0
, for any
K>0
, by (
5.24
), there are
N_{0}
large enough and
C_{3}>0
such that for all
N\geqslant N_{0}
,

\frac{\kappa_{5}|x|^{m+1}}{1+\sqrt{N^{-1}}|b_{1}(x)|^{1-\frac{1}{m}}}\leqslant\frac{\kappa_{5}|x|^{m+1}}{1+\sqrt{N^{-1}}(\kappa_{2}(1+|x|))^{m-1}}\leqslant K\kappa_{5}|x|^{2}+C_{3}.

Thus, for any
\kappa_{6}<0
, there is an
N_{0}
large enough such that for all
N\geqslant N_{0}
,

\displaystyle N\langle x,b_{N}[x,\mu]\rangle+N|b_{N}[x,\mu]|^{2}\leqslant\kappa_{6}|x|^{2}+\tfrac{c_{3}}{2}\mu(|\cdot|)^{2}+C_{4}.

(5.32)

For
{\mathcal{B}}^{N}_{\mu}(|\cdot|^{\beta})
, substituting (
5.32
) into (
5.10
), we get

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}^{N}_{\mu}(|\cdot|^{\beta})(x)

\displaystyle\leqslant\beta\Big(\kappa_{6}|x|^{2}+\tfrac{c_{3}}{2}\mu(|\cdot|)^{2}+C_{4}\Big)\int^{1}_{0}|x+\theta b_{N}[x,\mu]|^{\beta-2}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta.

On the other hand, for any
\varepsilon,\theta\in(0,1)
, by
|a+b|^{p}\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)|a|^{p}+C_{\varepsilon}|b|^{p}
, we have

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)|x|^{\beta-2}-C_{\varepsilon}|b_{N}[x,\mu]|^{\beta-2}\leqslant|x+\theta b_{N}[x,\mu]|^{\beta-2}\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)|x|^{\beta-2}-C^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}|b_{N}[x,\mu]|^{\beta-2}.

Moreover, for any
\delta>0
, by (
5.24
) and (
5.26
), there is an
N_{0}
large enough so that for all
N\geqslant N_{0}
,

\displaystyle|b_{N}[x,\mu]|\leqslant\frac{\kappa_{2}(1+|x|)}{N}+\frac{c_{1}+c_{2}|x|+c_{3}\mu(|\cdot|)}{N}\leqslant\delta(1+|x|+\mu(|\cdot|)).

(5.33)

Thus for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, one can choose
N_{0}
large enough so that for all
N\geqslant N_{0}
and
\theta\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)|x|^{\beta-2}-\varepsilon(1+\mu(|\cdot|)^{\beta-2})\leqslant|x+\theta b_{N}[x,\mu]|^{\beta-2}\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)|x|^{\beta-2}+\varepsilon(1+\mu(|\cdot|)^{\beta-2}).

Hence, for any
\kappa_{6}<0
,
there is an
N_{0}
large enough so that
for all
N\geqslant N_{0}
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}^{N}_{\mu}(|\cdot|^{\beta})(x)\leqslant\beta(\kappa_{6}|x|^{\beta}+c_{3}\mu(|\cdot|)^{\beta}+C_{5}).

(5.34)

For
{\mathcal{A}}^{N}_{\mu}U_{\beta}(x)
, as in the Step 2 of Lemma
3.12
and by (
5.33
), we have

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{A}}^{N}_{\mu}(|\cdot|^{\beta})(x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\sigma[x,\mu,z]|^{2}\int^{1}_{0}\theta\int^{1}_{-1}|x+\theta\theta^{\prime}N^{-1/2}\sigma[x,\mu,z]+b_{N}[x,\mu]|^{\beta-2}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta^{\prime}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\theta\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\sigma[x,\mu,z]|^{2}\Big(|x|^{\beta-2}+|\sigma[x,\mu,z]|^{\beta-2}+|b_{N}[x,\mu]|^{\beta-2}\Big)\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\kappa_{1}^{\beta}(|x|^{\beta}+\mu(|\cdot|)^{\beta}))\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}(1+|z|^{\beta})\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z),

which together with (
5.34
) and the arbitrariness of
\kappa_{6}<0
yields the desired estimate.
∎

Remark 5.9
.

When
m=1
, the similar estimate of (
5.31
) still hold, but parameter dependence becomes cumbersome.

We have the following corollary.

Corollary 5.10
.

Under
\rm({\bf\widetilde{H}}^{\sigma,b}_{\nu,\alpha})
, where
\alpha>2
, for any
\beta\in[2,\alpha)
and
T>0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{i=1,\cdots,N}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|X^{N,i}_{t}|^{\beta}\right)<\infty.

(5.35)

Moreover, if
m>1
, then for any
\kappa<0
, there is a constant
C>0
such that for all
t>0
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}{\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,i}_{t}|^{\beta}\leqslant\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\kappa t}}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}{\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,i}_{0}|^{\beta}+C,

(5.36)

and for any
i=1,\cdots,N
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,i}_{t}|^{\beta}\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{\kappa t}{\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,i}_{0}|^{\beta}+C\mathrm{e}^{\kappa t}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}{\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,j}_{0}|^{\beta}+C.

(5.37)

Proof.

For fixed
\beta\in[2,\alpha)
, by Itô’s formula (
5.2
) and (
5.30
), we have

\displaystyle|X^{N,i}_{t}|^{\beta}

\displaystyle=|X^{N,i}_{0}|^{\beta}+\int^{t}_{0}{\mathscr{L}}^{N}_{s,\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}}}(|\cdot|^{\beta})(X^{N,i}_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+M^{N,i}_{t}

(5.38)

\displaystyle\leqslant|X^{N,i}_{0}|^{\beta}+\int^{t}_{0}\Big(C_{0}|X^{N,i}_{s}|^{\beta}+C_{1}\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s}}(|\cdot|)^{\beta}+C_{2}\Big){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+M^{N,i}_{t},

where

M^{N,i}_{t}=\int^{t}_{0}\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\Theta^{N}_{\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}}}(|\cdot|^{\beta})(X^{N,i}_{s-},z)\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{N,i}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

is a local martingale.
Noting that

\displaystyle\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s}}(|\cdot|)^{\beta}\leqslant\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}|X^{N,j}_{s}|^{\beta}=:A^{N,\beta}_{s},

(5.39)

we have

A^{N,\beta}_{t}\leqslant A^{N,\beta}_{0}+(C_{0}+C_{1})\int^{t}_{0}A^{N,\beta}_{s}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+C_{2}t+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}M^{N,i}_{t}.

For any
q\in(0,1)
and
T>0
, by stochastic Gronwall’s inequality (see
[
41
, Lemma 3.7]
), we have

\sup_{N}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|A^{N,\beta}_{t}|^{q}\right)<\infty

and

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|X^{N,i}_{t}|^{\beta q}\right)\leqslant C\left({\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,i}_{0}|^{\beta}+C_{2}{\mathbb{E}}\int^{T}_{0}A^{N,\beta}_{s}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+C_{3}T\right)^{q}<\infty.

In particular,
M^{N,i}_{t}
is a martingale. If
m>1
, then by (
5.38
) and (
5.31
), for any
\kappa<0
,

\displaystyle{\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,i}_{t}|^{\beta}/{\mathord{{\rm d}}}t\leqslant(\kappa-(\beta c_{3}+C_{3}\kappa^{\beta}_{1})){\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,i}_{t}|^{\beta}+(\beta c_{3}+C_{3}\kappa^{\beta}_{1}){\mathbb{E}}A^{N,\beta}_{t}+C_{4},

and

{\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathbb{E}}A^{N,\beta}_{t}/{\mathord{{\rm d}}}t\leqslant\kappa{\mathbb{E}}A^{N,\beta}_{t}+C_{4}.

Solving these two differential inequalities, we obtain the desired estimates.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

If
m>1
, then by (
5.37
), as in Remark
5.6
one can show the existence of invariant probability measures for
the semigroup
P^{N}_{t}
defined through SDE (
5.1
).

The following lemma is similar to Lemma
5.4
.

Lemma 5.12
.

For any
T,\gamma>0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\sup_{N}\sup_{\tau\leqslant\eta\leqslant\tau+\delta\leqslant T}{\mathbb{P}}\Big(|X^{N,1}_{\eta}-X^{N,1}_{\tau}|\geqslant\gamma\Big)=0.

(5.40)

Proof.

Let
\tau,\eta\in{\mathscr{T}}_{T}
with
\tau\leqslant\eta\leqslant\tau+\delta
. For any
R>0
, define

\zeta_{R}:=\inf\left\{t>0:|X^{N,1}_{t}|\wedge A^{N,2}_{t}>R\right\},

where
A^{N,2}_{t}
is defined by (
5.39
), and

\tau_{R}:=\zeta_{R}\wedge\tau,\ \eta_{R}:=\zeta_{R}\wedge\eta.

By (
5.1
), we can write

\displaystyle X^{N,1}_{\eta_{R}}-X^{N,1}_{\tau_{R}}

\displaystyle=\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sigma_{N}{\Big[}s,X^{N,1}_{s-},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}},z{\Big]}{\mathcal{H}}^{N,1}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)

\displaystyle\quad+\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}b_{N}{\Big[}s,X^{N,1}_{s-},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}}{\Big]}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{N,1}_{s}=:I_{1}+I_{2}.

For
I_{1}
, by (
3.10
) and the isometry of stochastic integrals, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|I_{1}|^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left|\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\sigma_{N}{\Big[}s,X^{N,1}_{s-},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}},z{\Big]}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{N,1}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\Big|\sigma{\Big[}s,X^{N,1}_{s},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s}},z{\Big]}\Big|^{2}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s{\big)}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant{\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\!\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Big|\sigma(s,X^{N,1}_{s},X^{N,j}_{s},z)\Big|^{2}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}(1+|X^{N,1}_{s}|^{2}+A^{N,2}_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|z|^{2}\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)\leqslant C_{R}\delta.

For
I_{2}
, by (
5.24
) and (
5.26
) we similarly have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|I_{2}|

\displaystyle\leqslant{\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}|b_{N}\big[s,X^{N,1}_{s-},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}}\big]|{\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{N,1}_{s}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}\big|b_{N}\big[s,X^{N,1}_{s},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s}}\big]\big|{\mathord{{\rm d}}}(Ns)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant{\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}{\big(}\big|b_{1}{\big(}s,X^{N,1}_{s}{\big)}\big|+\big|b_{2}\big[s,X^{N,1}_{s},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s}}\big]\big|{\big)}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}\left(\int^{\eta_{R}}_{\tau_{R}}{\big(}1+|X^{N}_{s}|^{m}+A^{N,2}_{s}{\big)}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)\leqslant C_{R}\delta.

Hence, by Chebyshev’s inequality and (
5.35
),

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(|X^{N,1}_{\eta}-X^{N,1}_{\tau}|\geqslant\gamma)

\displaystyle\leqslant{\mathbb{P}}(|X^{N,1}_{\eta_{R}}-X^{N,1}_{\tau_{R}}|\geqslant\gamma;\zeta_{R}>T)+{\mathbb{P}}(\zeta_{R}\leqslant T)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{2}{\mathbb{P}}(|I_{i}|\geqslant\tfrac{\gamma}{3})+{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}(|X^{N,1}_{t}|\vee|A^{N,2}_{t}|\geqslant R\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant(\tfrac{3}{\gamma})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}|I_{1}|^{2}+\tfrac{3}{\gamma}{\mathbb{E}}|I_{2}|+\tfrac{C}{R}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{R,\gamma}\delta+C/R,

which converges to zero by firstly letting
\delta\to 0
and then
R\to\infty
.
∎

The following propagation of chaos result can be proven using the same methodology as presented in Theorem
5.5
. Due to the similarity of the arguments, we omit the detailed proof here.

Theorem 5.13
.

Let
\mu_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Suppose that for any
k\leqslant N
,

{\mathbb{P}}\circ{\big(}X^{N,1}_{0},\cdots,X^{N,k}_{0}{\big)}^{-1}\to\mu_{0}^{\otimes k},\ \ N\to\infty,

and DDSDE (
5.29
) admits a unique martingale solution
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\mu_{0}}_{0}({\mathscr{L}}^{\infty})

with initial distribution
\mu_{0}
in the sense of Definition
6.2
in appendix.
Then under
\rm(\widetilde{\bf H}^{\sigma,b}_{\nu,\alpha})
, for any
k\leqslant N
,

{\mathbb{P}}\circ{\big(}X^{N,1}_{\cdot},\cdots,X^{N,k}_{\cdot}{\big)}^{-1}\to{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{\otimes k},\ \ N\to\infty.

5.3.
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
-convergence rate under Lipschitz assumptions

In this section, we establish the quantitative convergence rate of the propagation of chaos phenomenon for the additive noise particle system given by:

\displaystyle X^{N,i}_{t}=X^{N,i}_{0}+N^{-1}\int^{t}_{0}b\big[s,X^{N,i}_{s-},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s-}}\big]{\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{N,i}_{s}+N^{-1/\alpha}H^{N,i}_{t},

(5.41)

where
\alpha\in(0,2]
,
{\mathcal{N}}^{N,i}
and
H^{N,i}
are the same as in the beginning of this section, and
b(s,x,y):{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
satisfies
that for some
\kappa>0
and for all
s,x,y,y^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle|b(s,x,y)|\leqslant\kappa,\ \ |b(s,x,y)-b(s,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})|\leqslant\kappa(|x-x^{\prime}|+|y-y^{\prime}|).

(5.42)

The associated limiting McKean-Vlasov SDE is given by

\displaystyle X_{t}=X_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}b[s,X_{s},\mu_{X_{s}}]{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+L^{(\alpha)}_{t}.

(5.43)

Under (
5.42
), it is well-known that (
5.43
) has a unique solution for any
\alpha\in(0,2)
. We aim to show the following result.

Theorem 5.14
.

Suppose that
\{X^{N,i}_{0},i=1,\cdots,N\}
are i.i.d.
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
-measurable random variables with common distribution
\mu_{0}
.
Under (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
) and (
5.42
), where
\alpha>1
, for any
T>0
, there is a constant
C=C(\kappa,\alpha,T,d,\|b\|_{\infty})>0
such that for all
t\in[0,T]
,

{\mathcal{W}}_{1}\big(\mu_{X^{N,1}_{t}},\mu_{X_{t}}\big)\leqslant C\Big(N^{-\frac{(\alpha-2)\wedge 1}{2}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha\in(2,3)}+N^{-\frac{2-\alpha}{2}\wedge\beta_{1}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha\in(1,2)}\Big),

where
\beta_{1}
is from (
H
{}^{\alpha}_{\nu}
) , and for two probability measures
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
denotes the Wasserstein 1-distance defined by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\sup_{\|\phi\|_{C^{1}_{b}}\leqslant 1}|\mu_{1}(\phi)-\mu_{2}(\phi)|.

(5.44)

Proof.

Let
\mu_{t}:=\mu_{X_{t}}
and
\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{t}
solve the following particle system:

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{t}=X^{N,i}_{0}+N^{-1}\int^{t}_{0}b\big[s,\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{s-},\mu_{s}\big]{\mathord{{\rm d}}}{\mathcal{N}}^{N,i}_{s}+N^{-1/\alpha}H^{N,i}_{t}.

(5.45)

Clearly,
\{\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{\cdot},i=1,\cdots,N\}
are i.i.d.
By (
5.41
) and (
5.45
), we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,i}_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{t}|

\displaystyle\leqslant{\mathbb{E}}\int^{t}_{0}\Big|b\big[s,X^{N,i}_{s},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s}}\big]-b\big[s,\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{s},\mu_{s}\big]\Big|{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

\displaystyle\leqslant{\mathbb{E}}\int^{t}_{0}\Big|b\big[s,X^{N,i}_{s},\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s}}\big]-b\big[s,\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{s},\mu_{\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s}}\big]\Big|{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

\displaystyle\quad+{\mathbb{E}}\int^{t}_{0}\Big|b\big[s,\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{s},\mu_{\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s}}\big]-b\big[s,\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{s},\mu_{s}\big]\Big|{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s=:I_{1}+I_{2}.

For
I_{1}
, by (
5.42
) we have

I_{1}\leqslant\frac{\kappa}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{0}\left({\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,i}_{s}-\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{s}|+{\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,j}_{s}-\widetilde{X}^{N,j}_{s}|\right){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s.

For
I_{2}
, since
\{\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{s},i=1,\cdots,N\}
are i.i.d., by (
2.27
), (
5.42
) and definition (
5.44
), we have

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\int^{t}_{0}\left({\mathbb{E}}\Big|b\big[s,\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{s},\mu_{\widetilde{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{s}}\big]-b\big[s,\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{s},\mu_{s}\big]\Big|^{2}\right)^{1/2}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\int^{t}_{0}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(\mu_{\widetilde{X}^{N,1}_{s}},\mu_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+\frac{\|b\|_{\infty}}{\sqrt{N}}.

On the other hand, by Theorem
3.19
and Remark
3.20
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,T]}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}(\mu_{\widetilde{X}^{N,1}_{s}},\mu_{s})\lesssim N^{-\frac{(\alpha-2)\wedge 1}{2}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha\in(2,3)}+N^{-\frac{2-\alpha}{2}\wedge\beta_{1}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha\in(1,2)}.

(5.46)

Combining the above calculations, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,i}_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{t}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\kappa}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{0}\left({\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,i}_{s}-\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{s}|+{\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,j}_{s}-\widetilde{X}^{N,j}_{s}|\right){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

\displaystyle\quad+C\Big(N^{-\frac{(\alpha-2)\wedge 1}{2}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha\in(2,3)}+N^{-\frac{2-\alpha}{2}\wedge\beta_{1}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha\in(1,2)}\Big),

which implies by Gronwall’s inequality that for all
t\in[0,T]
,

{\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,i}_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{t}|\lesssim\frac{\kappa}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int^{t}_{0}{\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,j}_{s}-\widetilde{X}^{N,j}_{s}|{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+N^{-\frac{(\alpha-2)\wedge 1}{2}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha\in(2,3)}+N^{-\frac{2-\alpha}{2}\wedge\beta_{1}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha\in(1,2)}

and

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}{\mathbb{E}}|X^{N,i}_{t}-\widetilde{X}^{N,i}_{t}|\lesssim N^{-\frac{(\alpha-2)\wedge 1}{2}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha\in(2,3)}+N^{-\frac{2-\alpha}{2}\wedge\beta_{1}}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\alpha\in(1,2)}.

These together with (
5.46
) yield the desired estimate.
∎

Remark 5.15
.

Based on the aforementioned convergence result, an interesting direction for future work is to investigate the convergence of the fluctuation of the empirical measure given by:

\eta^{N}_{t}:=\sqrt{N}(\mu_{{\mathbf{X}}^{N}_{t}}-\mu_{X_{t}}).

This corresponds to studying the central limit theorem for the particle system, which characterizes the asymptotic behavior of the fluctuations around the mean behavior.

6.
Appendix: Martingale solutions

In this section, we provide a brief overview of some key notions and results related to the martingale solutions associated with the operators
{\mathscr{L}}_{t}
. These concepts and results are well-known and can be found in Jacob-Shiryaev’s textbook
[
23
]
. We include them here for the convenience of the readers.

Let
{\mathbb{D}}:={\mathbb{D}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
be the space of all càdlàg functions from
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, which is endowed with the Skorokhod topology
(see
[
23
, p325]
for precise definition).
The canonical process in
{\mathbb{D}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
is defined by

w_{t}(\omega)=\omega_{t},\ \ \omega\in{\mathbb{D}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}).

Let
{\mathscr{B}}^{0}_{t}:=\sigma\{w_{s},s\leqslant t\}
be the natural filtration and
{\mathscr{B}}_{t}:=\cap_{s>t}{\mathscr{B}}^{0}_{s}
.
For
R>0
, we introduce

\tau_{R}(\omega):=\inf\big\{t>0:|\omega_{t}|\vee|\omega_{t-}|\geqslant R\big\},\ \ \omega\in{\mathbb{D}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),

and

\displaystyle J(\omega):=\big\{t>0:\omega(t)-\omega(t-)>0\big\},\ \ V(\omega):=\big\{R>0:\tau_{R}(\omega)<\tau_{R+}(\omega)\big\}

(6.1)

and

\displaystyle V^{\prime}(\omega):=\big\{R>0:\tau_{R}(\omega)\in J(\omega),|\omega(\tau_{R}(\omega)-)|=R\big\}.

(6.2)

It is well-known that
\tau_{R}
is an
{\mathscr{B}}^{0}_{t}
-stopping time, that is, for all
t\geqslant 0
,
\{\tau_{R}\leqslant t\}\in{\mathscr{B}}^{0}_{t}
.
Moreover, the function
R\mapsto\tau_{R}(\omega)
is nondecreasing and left continuous, and

J(\omega)
,
V(\omega)
and
V^{\prime}(\omega)
are at most countable (see
[
23
, p340, Lemma 2.10]
).
The following proposition can be found in

[
23
, p341, Propositions 2.11 and 2.12]
and
[
23
, p349, Lemma 3.12]
.

Proposition 6.1
.

For each
R,t>0
, the mappings
\omega\mapsto\tau_{R}(\omega)
and

\omega\to(w_{t\wedge\tau_{R}})(\omega)
are continuous with respect to the Skorokhod topology
at each point
\omega
such that
R\notin V(\omega)\cup V^{\prime}(\omega)
. Moreover, for any
{\mathbb{P}}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
,
the set
\{R>0:{\mathbb{P}}(\omega:R\in V(\omega)\cup V^{\prime}(\omega))>0\}
is at most countable.

Let
{\mathscr{L}}:=({\mathscr{L}}_{s})_{s\geqslant 0}
be a family of linear operators from
C_{c}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
to
C({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.
We introduce the following notion of martingale solutions (see
[
38
]
).

Definition 6.2
.

Let
s>0
and
\mu_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. We call a probability measure
{\mathbb{P}}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
a martingale solution
associated with
{\mathscr{L}}
and with initial distribution
\mu_{0}
at time
s
if
{\mathbb{P}}\circ w^{-1}_{s}=\mu_{0}
, and for all
f\in C^{2}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, the process

M_{t}:=f(w_{t})-f(w_{s})-\int^{t}_{s}{\mathscr{L}}_{r}f(w_{r}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r

is a local
{\mathscr{B}}_{t}
-martingale after time
s
under the probability measure
{\mathbb{P}}
. All the martingale solutions starting
from
\mu_{0}
at time
s
is denoted by
{\mathcal{M}}^{\mu_{0}}_{s}({\mathscr{L}})
. If
\mu_{0}=\delta_{x}
for some
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
we shall simply write
{\mathcal{M}}^{x}_{s}({\mathscr{L}})={\mathcal{M}}^{\delta_{x}}_{s}({\mathscr{L}})
. If the operator
{\mathscr{L}}
also depends on the probability measure
{\mathbb{P}}
itself,
then we shall call the probability measure
{\mathbb{P}}
a solution of nonlinear martingale problems.

First of all we present the following purely technical result.

Proposition 6.3
.

Suppose that for each

(s,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, there is a unique martingale solution
{\mathbb{P}}_{s,x}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{x}_{s}({\mathscr{L}})
so that for each measurable
A\subset{\mathbb{D}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,
(s,x)\mapsto{\mathbb{P}}_{s,x}(A)
is Borel measurable.
Then
\{{\mathbb{P}}_{s,x},(s,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\}
is a family of strong Markov probability measures.
If in addition,
{\mathscr{L}}
is a second order differential operator with the form:

{\mathscr{L}}_{s}f(x)=\mathrm{tr}(a(s,x)\cdot\nabla^{2}f(x))+b(s,x)\cdot\nabla f(x),

where
a:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\otimes{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is a symmetric matrix-valued locally bounded measurable function
and
b:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is a vector-valued locally bounded measurable function, then for each
(s,x)
,

{\mathbb{P}}_{s,x}
concentrates on the space of continuous functions.

Proof.

The statement that the uniqueness of martingale solutions implies the strong Markov property is a well-known result (see
[
38
, Theorem 6.2.2]
). We omit the details here.
Now, let us prove the second conclusion. Without loss of generality we assume
s=0
. To show that
{\mathbb{P}}_{0,x}

concentrates on the space of continuous functions, by Kolmogorov’s continuity criterion, it suffices to show that
for any
R,T>0
and
0\leqslant t_{0}<t_{1}\leqslant T
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{0,x}}|w_{t_{1}\wedge\tau_{R}}-w_{t_{0}\wedge\tau_{R}}|^{4}\leqslant C_{R}|t_{1}-t_{0}|^{2}.

(6.3)

Let
0\leqslant t_{0}<t_{1}\leqslant T
. Since
\tau_{R}\circ\theta_{t_{0}}=\tau_{R}-t_{0}
for
t_{0}<\tau_{R}
, we have

t_{1}\wedge\tau_{R}=t_{0}+(t_{1}-t_{0})\wedge(\tau_{R}\circ\theta_{t_{0}}),\ \ t_{0}<\tau_{R}.

Since
\{t_{0}<\tau_{R}\}\in{\mathscr{B}}_{t_{0}}
, by the Markov property one sees that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{0,x}}|w_{t_{1}\wedge\tau_{R}}-w_{t_{0}\wedge\tau_{R}}|^{4}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{0,x}}\Big[|w_{t_{1}\wedge\tau_{R}}-w_{t_{0}}|^{4}{\mathbbm{1}}_{t_{0}<\tau_{R}}\Big]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{0,x}}\Big[{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{0,x}}\Big(|w_{t_{0}+(t_{1}-t_{0})\wedge(\tau_{R}\circ\theta_{t_{0}})}-w_{t_{0}}|^{4}|{\mathscr{B}}_{t_{0}}\Big){\mathbbm{1}}_{t_{0}<\tau_{R}}\Big]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{0,x}}\Big[\Big({\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{s,y}}|w_{s+(t_{1}-t_{0})\wedge(\tau_{R}\circ\theta_{s})}-y|^{4}\Big)\big|_{(s,y)=(t_{0},w_{t_{0}})}{\mathbbm{1}}_{t_{0}<\tau_{R}}\Big].

(6.4)

Fix
y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
\beta\geqslant 1
. Define
f(x)=|x-y|^{2\beta}
.
Note that

\displaystyle{\mathscr{L}}_{s}f(x)

\displaystyle=2\beta|x-y|^{2(\beta-1)}\Big[\mathrm{tr}(a(s,x))+\langle x-y,b(s,x)\rangle\Big]

\displaystyle+4\beta(\beta-1)|x-y|^{2(\beta-2)}\langle a(s,x)(x-y),x-y\rangle.

In particular, for any
R>0
and
T>0
,

\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\sup_{|x|\leqslant R}|{\mathscr{L}}_{s}f(x)|\leqslant C_{R}(|x-y|^{2(\beta-1)}+|x-y|^{2\beta-1}).

Now for
s,t\in[0,T]
, by the definition of martingale solutions, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{s,y}}|w_{s+t\wedge(\tau_{R}\circ\theta_{s})}-y|^{2\beta}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{s,y}}f(w_{s+t\wedge(\tau_{R}\circ\theta_{s})})={\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{s,y}}\left(\int^{s+t\wedge(\tau_{R}\circ\theta_{s})}_{s}{\mathscr{L}}_{r}f(w_{r}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{R}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{s,y}}\left(\int^{s+t\wedge(\tau_{R}\circ\theta_{s})}_{s}\Big(|w_{r}-y|^{2(\beta-1)}+|w_{r}-y|^{2\beta-1}\Big){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right)

\displaystyle=C_{R}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{s,y}}\left(\int^{t\wedge(\tau_{R}\circ\theta_{s})}_{0}\Big(|w_{s+r}-y|^{2(\beta-1)}+|w_{s+r}-y|^{2\beta-1}\Big){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{R}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{s,y}}\left(\int^{t}_{0}\Big(|w_{s+r\wedge(\tau_{R}\circ\theta_{s})}-y|^{2(\beta-1)}+|w_{s+r\wedge(\tau_{R}\circ\theta_{s})}-y|^{2\beta}\Big){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right).

By Gronwall’s inequality, we get

{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{s,y}}|w_{s+t\wedge(\tau_{R}\circ\theta_{s})}-y|^{2\beta}\leqslant C_{R}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{s,y}}\left(\int^{t}_{0}|w_{s+r\wedge(\tau_{R}\circ\theta_{s})}-y|^{2(\beta-1)}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right).

In particular, if one takes
\beta=1
, then for any
s,t\in[0,T]
,

{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{s,y}}|w_{s+t\wedge(\tau_{R}\circ\theta_{s})}-y|^{2}\leqslant C_{R}t.

Furthermore, taking
\beta=2
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{s,y}}|w_{s+t\wedge(\tau_{R}\circ\theta_{s})}-y|^{4}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{R}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}_{s,y}}\left(\int^{t}_{0}|w_{s+r\wedge(\tau_{R}\circ\theta_{s})}-y|^{2}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right)\leqslant C_{R}t^{2}.

Substituting this into (
6
), we obtain (
6.3
). The proof is complete.
∎

Next we show a result that provides a way to construct a martingale solution for the operator
{\mathscr{L}}_{s}
.
Let
\{(X^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0},\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}

be a family of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
-valued càdlàg adapted processes on some stochastic basis

(\Omega^{\varepsilon},{\mathcal{F}}^{\varepsilon},{\mathbb{P}}^{\varepsilon};({\mathcal{F}}^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0})
. Let
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\varepsilon}
be the law of
X^{\varepsilon}
in
{\mathbb{D}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.
Let
\{{\mathscr{L}}^{\varepsilon}=({\mathscr{L}}^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0},\varepsilon\in(0,1)\}
be a family of
random
linear operators from
C^{\infty}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
to
C({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.
Suppose that

(H)

{\mathbb{Q}}_{\varepsilon}
weakly converges to
{\mathbb{Q}}_{0}
in
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{D}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, and for any
f\in C^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,

\displaystyle M^{\varepsilon}_{t}:=f(X^{\varepsilon}_{t})-f(X^{\varepsilon}_{0})-\int^{t}_{0}{\mathscr{L}}^{\varepsilon}_{s}f(X^{\varepsilon}_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

(6.5)

is a local
{\mathcal{F}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}
-martingale with localized stopping time sequence
(\tau^{\varepsilon}_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, where for each
R>0
,

\tau^{\varepsilon}_{R}:=\inf\big\{t>0:|X^{\varepsilon}_{t}|\vee|X^{\varepsilon}_{t-}|\geqslant R\big\}.

Moreover, for each
t,R>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}^{\varepsilon}}\left|\int^{t\wedge\tau^{\varepsilon}_{R}}_{0}({\mathscr{L}}^{\varepsilon}_{s}f-{\mathscr{L}}_{s}f)(X^{\varepsilon}_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right|=0.

(6.6)

We have the following result about the martingale solutions.

Theorem 6.4
.

Under
(H)
, it holds that
{\mathbb{Q}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\mu_{0}}_{0}({\mathscr{L}})
, where
\mu_{0}:={\mathbb{Q}}_{0}\circ w_{0}^{-1}
.

Proof.

For given
f\in C^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, define

\displaystyle M_{t}:=f(w_{t})-f(w_{0})-\int^{t}_{0}{\mathscr{L}}_{s}f(w_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s.

(6.7)

Recall the definitions
V(\omega)
and
V^{\prime}(\omega)
in (
6.1
) and (
6.2
).
Since
{\mathbb{T}}:=\{R>0:{\mathbb{Q}}_{0}(\omega:R\in V(\omega)\cup V^{\prime}(\omega))>0\}
is at most countable
and
\lim_{R\to\infty}\tau_{R}\to\infty
,
to show
{\mathbb{Q}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\mu_{0}}_{0}({\mathscr{L}})
, it suffices to show that for each
R\in{\mathbb{T}}
and
s<t
,

{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{Q}}_{0}}\big(M_{t\wedge\tau_{R}}|{\mathscr{B}}_{s\wedge\tau_{R}}\big)=M_{s\wedge\tau_{R}},

or equivalently, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
g\in C_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{nd})
and
s_{1}<s_{2}<\cdots<s_{n}\leqslant s_{0}
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{Q}}_{0}}\Big[\big(M_{t\wedge\tau_{R}}-M_{s\wedge\tau_{R}}\big)G(w_{\cdot\wedge\tau_{R}})\Big]=0,

(6.8)

where
G(w):=g(w_{s_{1}},\cdots,w_{s_{n}}).

Note that by the assumption,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}^{\varepsilon}}\Big[\big(M^{\varepsilon}_{t\wedge\tau^{\varepsilon}_{R}}-M^{\varepsilon}_{s\wedge\tau^{\varepsilon}_{R}}\big)G(X^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot\wedge\tau^{\varepsilon}_{R}})\Big]=0,

(6.9)

where
M^{\varepsilon}_{t}
is defined by (
6.5
) and
\tau^{\varepsilon}_{R}:=\inf\big\{t>0:|X^{\varepsilon}_{t}|\vee|X^{\varepsilon}_{t-}|\geqslant R\big\}
.
We want to take weak limits.
Since by Proposition
6.1
,

{\mathbb{D}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\ni\omega\mapsto\Big[\big(f(w_{t\wedge\tau_{R}})-f(w_{s\wedge\tau_{R}})\big)G(w_{\cdot\wedge\tau_{R}})\Big](\omega)=:H(\omega)\in{\mathbb{R}}

is bounded and
{\mathbb{Q}}_{0}
-a.s. continuous, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\varepsilon}}H={\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{Q}}_{0}}H.

Thus, by definitions (
6.5
) and (
6.7
), to prove (
6.8
), it remains to show

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}^{\varepsilon}}\left(G(X^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot\wedge\tau^{\varepsilon}_{R}})\int^{t\wedge\tau^{\varepsilon}_{R}}_{s\wedge\tau^{\varepsilon}_{R}}{\mathscr{L}}^{\varepsilon}_{r}f(X^{\varepsilon}_{r}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right)={\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{Q}}_{0}}\left(G(w_{\cdot\wedge\tau_{R}})\int^{t\wedge\tau_{R}}_{s\wedge\tau_{R}}{\mathscr{L}}_{r}f(w_{r}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right).

(6.10)

Since for each
r
,
x\mapsto{\mathscr{L}}_{r}f(x)
is a continuous function, by Proposition
6.1
, one sees that

{\mathbb{D}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\ni\omega\mapsto\left(G(w_{\cdot\wedge\tau_{R}})\int^{t\wedge\tau_{R}}_{s\wedge\tau_{R}}{\mathscr{L}}_{r}f(w_{r}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right)(\omega)\in{\mathbb{R}}

is bounded and
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
-a.s. continuous. Thus,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{P}}^{\varepsilon}}\left(G(X^{\varepsilon}_{\cdot\wedge\tau^{\varepsilon}_{R}})\int^{t\wedge\tau^{\varepsilon}_{R}}_{s\wedge\tau^{\varepsilon}_{R}}{\mathscr{L}}_{r}f(X^{\varepsilon}_{r}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\varepsilon}}\left(G(w_{\cdot\wedge\tau_{R}})\int^{t\wedge\tau_{R}}_{s\wedge\tau_{R}}{\mathscr{L}}_{r}f(w_{r}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbb{Q}}_{0}}\left(G(w_{\cdot\wedge\tau_{R}})\int^{t\wedge\tau_{R}}_{s\wedge\tau_{R}}{\mathscr{L}}_{r}f(w_{r}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right),

which together with (
6.6
) yields (
6.10
). The proof is complete.
∎

2.3
